Schauder-type Estimates and Log-Critical Well-posedness for the Two-Phase Muskat Problem with Surface Tension
Ke Chen, Ruilin Hu, Quoc-Hung Nguyen

Abstract
We prove short-time well-posedness for the Muskat problem with surface tension in the full two-phase setting, allowing different viscosities, arbitrary density contrast, and rigid boundaries. In particular, no Rayleigh–Taylor sign condition on the density contrast is imposed. The interface is assumed to be a graph, uniformly separated from the fixed boundaries, and the initial data may be large in the log-critical class C ˙ 1 , log ϰ ∩ H 1 \dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}\cap H^{1} , with ϰ > 1 \varkappa>1 . Thus the result reaches the natural Lipschitz threshold up to a logarithmic correction. The main difficulty is that, in the presence of viscosity jump and boundaries, the interface equation is not given by a closed explicit contour dynamics law. Instead, the normal velocity is recovered through an elliptic transmission problem in moving domains, and the resulting evolution is a genuinely nonlocal quasilinear equation. We derive sharp Schauder-type estimates, adapted to the log-critical scale, for the transmission operators generated by the bulk Darcy flow. These estimates identify the third-order parabolic mechanism produced by surface tension and control the nonlinear coupling between the interface geometry and the elliptic transmission structure. The proof builds on the Schauder framework developed in Part I of this series [ 18 ] , but requires a new analysis of the Muskat transmission problem in moving domains. Combining this elliptic theory with the contour formulation and time-weighted Hölder estimates, we obtain existence, uniqueness, smoothing, and stability for large interfaces in arbitrary dimension.



1
Introduction

In this paper, we study the Muskat problem with surface tension in the general two-phase setting, allowing both viscosity jump and rigid boundaries. The interface is represented as the graph of a function
\eta(t,x)
, and we address the Cauchy problem for large initial data near the critical Lipschitz threshold. Our main result establishes short-time well-posedness for initial interfaces in the logarithmically refined critical space

\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}\cap H^{1},\qquad\varkappa>1,

under the natural geometric assumption that the interface remains uniformly separated from the fixed boundaries.

The Muskat problem
[
46
]
describes the evolution of the interface between two immiscible fluids in a porous medium. This model has significant applications in various fields including petroleum engineering, groundwater hydrology, and environmental sciences. When the interface is represented as a graph of a time-dependent function
\eta(t,x)
, the system can be formulated as a free boundary problem governed by Darcy’s law with appropriate boundary conditions at the interface and rigid boundaries.

In the physical configuration, consider two fluids with densities
\rho^{\pm}
and viscosities
\mu^{\pm}
occupying domains
\Omega_{t}^{\pm}
separated by an interface
\Sigma_{t}=\{(x,\eta(t,x)):x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
.

Figure 1:
The Muskat problem

The domains are given by

\displaystyle\Omega_{t}^{+}=\left\{(x,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}:\eta(t,x)<z<\underline{b}^{+}(x)\right\},

\displaystyle\Omega_{t}^{-}=\left\{(x,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}:\underline{b}^{-}(x)<z<\eta(t,x)\right\},

where
\underline{b}^{\pm}\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
are the parameterizations of the rigid boundaries

\Gamma^{\pm}=\left\{\left(x,\underline{b}^{\pm}(x)\right):x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\right\}.

The incompressible fluid velocity
u^{\pm}
in each region is governed by Darcy’s law
[
29
]

\displaystyle\mu_{\pm}u^{\pm}+\nabla_{x,z}p^{\pm}=-\rho^{\pm}\mathfrak{g}\vec{e}_{d+1},\quad\quad\quad\operatorname{div}_{x,z}u^{\pm}=0\quad\text{ in }\Omega^{\pm}(t).

Here
p^{\pm}
is the pressure, and
\mathfrak{g}
is the Earth’s gravity. In this section, we simply denote
\nabla=\nabla_{x}
, and
\nabla_{x,z}=(\nabla,\partial_{z})
.
The normal velocity is continuous at the interface

\displaystyle u^{+}\cdot n=u^{-}\cdot n,

where
n=\langle\nabla\eta\rangle^{-1}(-\nabla\eta,1)
and
\langle a\rangle=\sqrt{1+|a|^{2}}
.
The pressure jump at the interface is proportional to the mean curvature, which incorporates the effect of surface tension (capillarity):

\displaystyle p^{-}-p^{+}=-\sigma\operatorname{div}\left(\frac{\nabla\eta}{\langle\nabla\eta\rangle}\right),\ \ \ \text{on}\ \Sigma_{t}.

For simplicity in the subsequent formulation, we assume the surface tension coefficient
\sigma=1
. The interface moves with the fluid

\partial_{t}\eta=\left.\langle\nabla\eta\rangle u^{+}\cdot n\right|_{\Sigma_{t}}=\left.\mathbf{n}\cdot u^{+}\right|_{\Sigma_{t}},

where we denote
\mathbf{n}=\langle\nabla\eta\rangle n
.
At the two rigid boundaries, the no-penetration boundary conditions are imposed

\displaystyle u^{\pm}\cdot\nu^{\pm}=0,\ \ \text{on}\ \Gamma^{\pm},

where
\nu^{\pm}=\frac{1}{\langle\nabla\underline{b}^{\pm}\rangle}(-\nabla\underline{b}^{\pm},1)
.

Introducing the modified pressures

\displaystyle q^{\pm}(x,z)=p^{\pm}(x,z)+\rho^{\pm}\mathfrak{g}z,

\displaystyle\varrho_{0}=(\rho^{-}-\rho^{+})\mathfrak{g},

the system can be rewritten as the elliptic transmission problem

\displaystyle\Delta_{x,z}q^{\pm}=0,\ \ \text{in}\ \Omega^{\pm}(t),

(1.1)

\displaystyle q^{-}-q^{+}=-\operatorname{div}\left(\frac{\nabla\eta}{\langle\nabla\eta\rangle}\right)+\varrho_{0}\eta,\ \ \text{on}\ \Sigma_{t},

\displaystyle\mathbf{n}\cdot\nabla_{x,z}\left(\frac{1}{\mu_{+}}q^{+}-\frac{1}{\mu_{-}}q^{-}\right)=0,\ \ \text{on}\ \Sigma_{t},

\displaystyle\nu^{\pm}\cdot\nabla_{x,z}q^{\pm}=0,\ \ \text{on}\ \Gamma^{\pm}.

The evolution equation of the interface reads

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\eta=-\frac{1}{\mu_{+}}\left.\mathbf{n}\cdot\nabla_{x,z}q^{+}\right|_{\Sigma_{t}}.

(1.2)

In the infinite-depth case, where no rigid boundaries are present, the graph equation enjoys a natural scaling symmetry:

\eta(t,x)\mapsto\eta_{\lambda}(t,x):=\lambda^{-1}\eta(\lambda^{3}t,\lambda x),\qquad\lambda>0.

Consequently, both

\dot{W}^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\dot{H}^{1+d/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

are scale-invariant spaces for the interface.

1.1
Related works

The well-posedness and regularity theory for the Muskat problem has been extensively studied under a variety of physical assumptions and analytic frameworks.

For the two-phase Muskat problem, in the absence of surface tension, the stability of the problem is governed by the Rayleigh–Taylor condition, which, in its simplest form, requires the denser fluid to lie below the lighter one
[
35
]
. If this condition fails, the interface may become unstable, leading to ill-posedness or finite-time singularity formation
[
50
]
. Even in initially stable configurations, smooth solutions can develop vertical tangents, known as “turning waves”, and subsequently lose the Rayleigh–Taylor stability condition in finite time
[
15
,
16
]
. We refer to the surveys of Gancedo
[
40
]
and Granero-Belinchón–Lazar
[
41
]
for further discussion of singularity formation.

In the stable regime without surface tension, global solutions have been constructed in various settings; see, for instance,
[
12
,
13
,
19
,
20
,
22
,
21
,
30
,
26
,
27
]
. Foundational local well-posedness results include the works of Yi
[
53
]
, Ambrose
[
9
,
10
]
, Córdoba and Gancedo
[
24
,
25
]
, and Cheng, Granero-Belinchón, and Shkoller
[
19
]
. More recently, local well-posedness in subcritical spaces was obtained by Constantin–Gancedo–Shvydkoy–Vicol
[
22
]
in Sobolev spaces
W^{2,p}(\mathbb{R})
, by Abels–Matioc
[
1
]
in
W^{s,p}
with
s>1+1/p
, by Matioc
[
43
,
44
]
, and by Alazard–Lazar
[
4
]
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
with
s>3/2
.

We next recall several developments at critical regularity. The first results in this direction concerned small-data solutions in Wiener-type spaces. Constantin, Córdoba, Gancedo, and Strain
[
20
]
established global small-data solutions without viscosity jump, and this was later extended to three dimensions in joint work with Piazza
[
21
]
. Gancedo, García-Juárez, Patel, and Strain
[
37
]
further developed this theory and constructed global small-data strong solutions allowing viscosity jump in both two and three dimensions.

There has also been substantial progress in critical Sobolev spaces. Córdoba and Lazar
[
28
]
, while working in the subcritical space
\dot{H}^{5/2}
, derived a priori estimates at the critical level
\dot{H}^{3/2}
. This was later extended to three dimensions by Gancedo and Lazar
[
36
]
. The first fully critical result was obtained by Alazard and the third author, who constructed two-dimensional solutions with initial data in
H^{3/2}\cap\dot{W}^{1,\infty}

[
7
]
; see also the log-subcritical work
[
6
]
, where unbounded slopes are allowed. Depending on the size of the data, these results yield either local well-posedness for large data or global well-posedness for small data. In subsequent work, the
L^{\infty}
slope assumption was removed, yielding well-posedness directly in
H^{3/2}

[
5
]
. This analysis was later extended to three dimensions in
\dot{H}^{2}\cap W^{1,\infty}

[
8
]
.

Further critical small-data results include the works of Cameron, who first studied well-posedness in
\dot{W}^{1,\infty}\cap L^{2}
in two dimensions
[
12
]
, and later in three dimensions under merely sublinear growth at infinity
[
13
]
. A notable feature of Cameron’s results is that they allow medium-sized data, in the sense that the slope is bounded by
1
rather than by a perturbatively small constant. Huy Nguyen
[
49
]
established well-posedness in the Besov space
\dot{B}_{\infty,1}^{1}
, which embeds into the critical Lipschitz class
\dot{W}^{1,\infty}
. For genuinely large data, global regularity is not expected in general due to possible singularity formation. In one space dimension, under a monotonicity assumption on the initial interface, Deng–Lei–Lin
[
30
]
constructed global weak solutions. Local well-posedness in the critical Hölder space
C^{1}
was established by the first and third authors together with Xu
[
17
]
. More recently,
[
14
]
obtained local existence of classical solutions in regimes where the product of maximal and minimal slopes is allowed to be large in arbitrary dimension. We also mention the work of García-Juárez, Gómez-Serrano, Huy Nguyen, and Pausader
[
38
]
on small self-similar solutions emanating from exact corner singularities, as well as
[
39
]
on the desingularization of small moving corners.

The inclusion of surface tension introduces the mean-curvature term, which yields a third-order parabolic mechanism for graph interfaces. We recall the results most relevant to the surface-tension problem. Huy Nguyen
[
47
]
and Huy Nguyen–Pausader
[
48
]
proved local well-posedness in any subcritical Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
s>1+\frac{d}{2}
, considering both one fluid or two fluids settings, with or without viscosity jump, with or without rigid boundaries. The proof employed a paradifferential approach for the Dirichlet-Neumann operator, see also
[
2
,
3
]
for related studies in the context of water waves. In two dimensions, A.-V. Matioc and B.-V. Matioc
[
45
]
introduced a reformulation based on layer-potential operators and obtained local well-posedness with surface tension and general viscosities in subcritical
L^{p}
based Sobolev spaces. More recently, Lazar
[
42
]
proved a global well-posedness result for the 2D surface-tension problem without viscosity jump, with any regular enough initial data which is small in some critical space but possibly large in Lipschitz. The general two-phase problem with viscosity jump and rigid boundaries leads to a different difficulty, since the normal velocity is recovered through an elliptic transmission problem rather than an explicit closed contour equation. We also mention the recent work of Wan and Yang

[
51
]
, where the two-dimensional Muskat problem with
an elastic interface was studied via a Dirichlet–Neumann formulation.
Their model exhibits a higher-order quasilinear parabolic structure, in
contrast to the surface-tension-driven problem considered here.

For the one-phase Muskat problem, a remarkable global theory for large
Lipschitz graphs was recently developed by Dong, Gancedo and Huy Nguyen
[
32
]
.
In two space dimensions, they proved global well-posedness for arbitrary
periodic Lipschitz initial interfaces by combining quantitative estimates
for layer potentials with pointwise elliptic regularity in Lipschitz
domains. This theory was subsequently extended to the three-dimensional
one-phase problem
[
33
]
, where the analysis relies on delicate structural
properties of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map and layer potentials in
Lipschitz graph domains. In the presence of surface
tension, Dong and Kwon
[
34
]
recently proved global
well-posedness for small one-phase data in
H^{s}
with
s>d/2+1
.

In the present work, we study the Muskat problem with surface tension in the general two-phase setting illustrated in Figure
1
, allowing viscosity jump and rigid boundaries. Our aim is to develop a well-posedness theory near the critical Lipschitz threshold for large interfaces, a delicate issue already highlighted in
[
48
]
.

The main difficulty is that the interface equation (
1.2
) is not a closed local equation for
\eta
. Rather, the normal velocity is determined implicitly through the elliptic transmission problem (
1.1
) in moving domains. Consequently, the resulting interface operator is both nonlocal and quasilinear. In the presence of viscosity jump and rigid boundaries, the structural simplifications available in the equal-viscosity contour-dynamics formulation are no longer present. The key task is therefore to obtain sharp regularity estimates for the transmission operators associated with the bulk Darcy flow and to couple these estimates with the interface evolution.

Our analysis is rooted in the Schauder framework developed in Part I of this series
[
18
]
, where a general theory was established for parabolic equations governed by nonlocal pseudo-differential operators. We also refer to the work of Constantin, Ignatova, and the third author
[
23
]
, where a related freezing-coefficient strategy was used to study the critical SQG equation in bounded domains and to establish global regularity. The present work, however, requires several new ingredients specific to the Muskat transmission problem. We first recast the free-boundary system as a contour-dynamics equation for the interface, in which the normal velocity is determined implicitly through an elliptic transmission problem in moving domains. We then identify the third-order parabolic principal part generated by surface tension and prove Schauder-type estimates, in the logarithmically refined critical scale, for the elliptic transmission operators associated with the bulk Darcy flow. These estimates allow us to close the nonlinear argument in the class
1
1

1

On
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, the condition

h\in\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}\cap H^{1}
is equivalent to

h\in C^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}
, since the
H^{1}
norm controls the
zeroth-order part and periodicity removes the homogeneous ambiguity.

\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}\cap H^{1},\qquad\varkappa>1,

and to prove short-time well-posedness in arbitrary dimension.

1.2
Main result

For
\beta\in[0,1),\varkappa>0
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, define

\displaystyle\|h\|_{\dot{C}^{\beta,\log^{\varkappa}}}:=\sup_{x\neq y}\frac{|h(x)-h(y)|}{\log^{-\varkappa}\left(2+\frac{1}{|x-y|}\right)|x-y|^{\beta}},

(1.3)

\displaystyle\|h\|_{\dot{C}^{k+\beta,\log^{\varkappa}}}=\|\nabla^{k}h\|_{\dot{C}^{\beta,\log^{\varkappa}}}.

Since

\log^{-\varkappa}\left(2+\frac{1}{|x-y|}\right)\leq C_{\varkappa},

we obtain

\|h\|_{\dot{C}^{\beta}}\lesssim_{\varkappa}\|h\|_{\dot{C}^{\beta,\log^{\varkappa}}}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\log^{-\varkappa}\left(2+\frac{1}{|x-y|}\right)\to 0~~\text{as}~~|x-y|\to 0,

so
\dot{C}^{\beta,\log^{\varkappa}}
is a logarithmic refinement of the usual Hölder space

\dot{C}^{\beta}
at small scales. In particular, the following inclusion is strict:

\dot{C}^{\beta,\log^{\varkappa}}\subsetneq\dot{C}^{\beta}.

We also use the shorthand notation

\|h\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}=\|h\|_{\dot{C}^{0,\log^{\varkappa}}}.

For later use, we record that, for every
\varkappa>1,

\int_{|\alpha|<1}\|\delta_{\alpha}f\|_{L^{\infty}}\frac{d\alpha}{|\alpha|^{d}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\varkappa-1}\|f\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}.

We now state the main result of the paper.

Theorem 1.1

Let
\varkappa>1
,
n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
. Assume that

\eta_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{2\varkappa-1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

satisfies the separation condition

\operatorname{dist}(\eta_{0},\Gamma^{\pm})>2\mathbf{r}>0.

Then there exists a time

T=T(\eta_{0},\varkappa,\mathbf{r},m)>0,

such that the system (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) admits a unique solution

\eta\in C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\dot{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).

Moreover, this solution satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\|\eta(t)\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}+t^{\frac{m}{3}}|\log t|^{\varkappa}\|\nabla\eta(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{m}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\|\eta_{0}\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}},

(1.4)

\displaystyle\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\operatorname{dist}(\eta(t),\Gamma^{\pm})

\displaystyle>\mathbf{r},

(1.5)

for some constant
C>0
.

Remark 1.2

Our method also applies, with only minor modifications, to the Muskat problem without surface tension in the Rayleigh–Taylor stable regime. In this setting, the curvature term is absent and the leading-order dynamics are governed by a first-order parabolic operator generated by the density contrast through the elliptic transmission problem. Combining the freezing-coefficient argument with the corresponding first-order Schauder estimates yields local well-posedness for initial data in

H^{1}\cap C^{1,\log^{2\varkappa-1}},\qquad\varkappa>1,

under the same separation condition.

The estimate (
1.4
) reflects the regularizing effect of surface tension, while (
1.5
) guarantees that the interface
\Sigma_{t}
remains uniformly separated from the rigid boundaries
\Gamma^{\pm}
(see Figure
1
).

The regularity assumption in Theorem
1.1
is logarithmically critical. More precisely, compared with the critical Lipschitz class, we require the additional logarithmic regularity

f_{0}\in\dot{C}^{1,\log^{2\varkappa-1}},\qquad\varkappa>1.

This condition is needed to control singular integral terms generated by the viscosity jump. Our method does not appear to close in the pure Lipschitz class.

To illustrate the difficulty, consider the two-dimensional Muskat problem without rigid boundaries, which admits the contour dynamics formulation (see
[
43
]
)

\partial_{t}f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1+\partial_{x}f(x)\Delta_{\alpha}f(x)}{\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha}f(x)\right\rangle^{2}}\omega(x-\alpha)\frac{d\alpha}{\alpha},

(1.6)

where

\Delta_{\alpha}f(x)=\frac{f(x)-f(x-\alpha)}{\alpha},

and the vorticity density
\omega
is determined by

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}\partial_{x}\left(\frac{\partial_{x}^{2}f}{\langle\partial_{x}f\rangle^{3}}\right)-\varrho_{0}\partial_{x}f(x)=\omega(x)+(\mu_{-}-\mu_{+})K(f)(\omega)(x),

(1.7)

\displaystyle K(f)(\omega)(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\partial_{x}f(x)-\Delta_{\alpha}f(x)}{\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha}f(x)\right\rangle^{2}}\omega(x-\alpha)\frac{d\alpha}{\alpha}.

Here
\tilde{\sigma}>0
denotes the surface tension coefficient and

\varrho_{0}=(\rho^{-}-\rho^{+})\mathfrak{g}

is proportional to the density contrast. The regime
\varrho_{0}>0
corresponds to the Rayleigh–Taylor stable configuration, whereas
\varrho_{0}<0
corresponds to the unstable regime.

When the viscosities coincide, namely
\mu_{-}=\mu_{+}
, the operator
K(f)
disappears and (
1.7
) reduces to the equation studied in
[
18
]
. In contrast, when
\mu_{-}\neq\mu_{+}
, the term
K(f)(\omega)
acts as a genuinely nonlinear perturbation of the surface-tension dynamics.

The leading dissipative mechanism is still provided by the surface-tension operator

\partial_{x}\Bigl(\frac{\partial_{x}^{2}f}{\langle\partial_{x}f\rangle^{3}}\Bigr),

which is of third order. To derive a priori estimates, one must control terms involving

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1+\partial_{x}f(x)\Delta_{\alpha}f(x)}{\langle\Delta_{\alpha}f(x)\rangle^{2}}\,K(f)(\omega)(x-\alpha)\,\frac{d\alpha}{\alpha}.

At the linearized level, this requires controlling the singular coefficient

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\partial_{x}f(x)-\Delta_{\alpha}f(x)}{\langle\Delta_{\alpha}f(x)\rangle^{2}}\,\frac{d\alpha}{\alpha}.

Since

\partial_{x}f(x)-\Delta_{\alpha}f(x)=O\!\left(\log^{-\varkappa}\!\left(2+\frac{1}{|\alpha|}\right)\right),

for
f\in\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}
, the above integral is convergent precisely when
\varkappa>1
. This logarithmic gain is therefore sufficient to make the viscosity-jump contribution integrable, whereas the corresponding integral is only borderline convergent in the pure Lipschitz class. This provides a heuristic explanation for the appearance of the space
\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}
in our analysis.

Several difficulties are specific to the general Muskat setting. First, the evolution equation is not local and not closed at the level of the interface: the normal velocity must be reconstructed from an elliptic transmission problem in domains depending on
\eta
. Second, when viscosity jump and rigid boundaries are present, one loses the structural simplifications available in the equal-viscosity contour-dynamics formulation, so the interface equation involves more intricate nonlocal operators. Third, the analysis is carried out at critical regularity, where one cannot rely on classical Lipschitz estimates and must instead work in the logarithmically corrected space
\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}
, with
\varkappa>1
. Finally, it is essential to propagate the separation condition between the interface and the rigid boundaries, since otherwise the elliptic transmission problem degenerates and the contour formulation ceases to be valid.

The proof of Theorem
1.1
begins with a reformulation of the free-boundary problem as a nonlocal evolution equation for the interface
\eta
. This equation is obtained by solving the elliptic transmission problem for the modified pressures
q^{\pm}
in the moving domains
\Omega_{t}^{\pm}
. Unlike in the equal-viscosity case, the resulting evolution is not a closed contour equation with an explicit leading operator. The normal velocity is instead determined implicitly through the bulk Darcy flow, and the corresponding interface operator depends nonlinearly both on the geometry of the interface and on the transmission structure across
\Sigma_{t}
.

A central step of the proof is to extract the leading surface-tension contribution in a form adapted to this logarithmically critical analysis. We derive a freezing-coefficient contour formulation of the interface equation. Schematically,

\partial_{t}\eta-\mathcal{L}_{\nabla\eta}\eta=\mathcal{R}[\eta]+N[\eta],

where
\mathcal{L}_{\nabla\eta}
is a third-order nonlocal parabolic operator with coefficients frozen at the local slope
\nabla\eta(x)
. More precisely, its principal part is given by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\eta(x)-\frac{\tilde{c}_{d}\langle\nabla\eta(x)\rangle^{2}}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{B(\nabla\eta(x)):\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{2}\eta(x)}{\langle\hat{\alpha}\cdot\nabla\eta(x)\rangle^{d+1}}\frac{d\alpha}{|\alpha|^{d+1}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{\tilde{c}_{d}\langle\nabla\eta(x)\rangle^{2}}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\delta_{\alpha}B(\nabla\eta)(x):\nabla^{2}\eta(x-\alpha)}{\langle\hat{\alpha}\cdot\nabla\eta(x)\rangle^{d+1}}\frac{d\alpha}{|\alpha|^{d+1}}+N[\eta](x),

(1.8)

as shown in Lemma
3.4
, where
\tilde{c}_{d}>0
is a constant and

B(b)=\frac{\mathrm{Id}}{\langle b\rangle}-\frac{b\otimes b}{\langle b\rangle^{3}}.

(1.9)

In dimension
d=1
, this principal operator has the leading behavior

\frac{\tilde{c}_{d}}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}}\frac{|D|^{3}\eta}{\langle\partial_{x}\eta\rangle^{3}}.

The term
N[\eta]
in (
1.2
) contains the remaining nonlinear and transmission contributions. Thus the contour formulation separates the leading dissipative singular operator from the lower-order transmission errors.

This separation is essential at logarithmically critical regularity. One cannot close the estimates by treating the full transmission operator as an unstructured nonlocal nonlinearity. Instead, we exploit the explicit third-order parabolic principal operator and estimate
N[\eta]
using sharp Schauder-type bounds for elliptic transmission problems in moving domains.

A second main ingredient is therefore an endpoint regularity theory for such transmission problems. After flattening the interface and freezing the geometry, we are led to divergence-form elliptic systems with coefficients depending only on the tangential variable and with jump conditions across a flat interface. More precisely, set

\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}=\begin{cases}\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\tau),&\tau>0,\\
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\tau,0),&\tau<0.\end{cases}

Let

A(x)\in C^{\log^{\varkappa}}\bigl(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{(d+1)\times(d+1)}_{\rm sym}\bigr)

be uniformly elliptic, and set

\mu(z)=\mu_{+}\mathbf{1}_{z\geq 0}+\mu_{-}\mathbf{1}_{z<0}.

We consider the model transmission problem

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}_{x,z}\bigl(\mu(z)^{-1}A(x)\nabla_{x,z}u\bigr)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}_{x,z}\bigl(\mu(z)^{-1}g\bigr),

\displaystyle\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r},

\displaystyle[u]_{z=0}

\displaystyle=h,

\displaystyle\text{on }\{z=0\},

\displaystyle e_{d+1}\cdot\bigl[\mu(z)^{-1}(A(x)\nabla_{x,z}u-g)\bigr]_{z=0}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\text{on }\{z=0\},

where

[w]_{z=0}=w(0^{-})-w(0^{+}).

For this model problem, we prove a log-Dini Lipschitz estimate of the form

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{x,z}u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{r/2}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-r/2})}\leq

\displaystyle C\|h\|_{C^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C\|g\|_{C^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}

\displaystyle+C\Bigl(\|\nabla_{x,z}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}\Bigr),

see Lemma
6.3
for the precise statement. This endpoint estimate gives the required control of the transmission error
N[\eta]
in the critical topology.

With the parabolic principal structure and the endpoint transmission estimates in hand, we derive a priori bounds in

\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}\cap H^{1},

together with time-weighted higher-order Hölder estimates.
The stronger assumption
\eta_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{2\varkappa-1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in Theorem
1.1
is used only to construct a smooth reference profile
\phi
such that

\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}\cap H^{1}}

(1.10)

is sufficiently small. Around this reference profile,
the principal operator is frozen and the transmission terms are estimated. We then solve the approximate problems, obtain estimates uniform in the approximation parameter, and pass to the limit by compactness. Finally, uniqueness follows from stability estimates for the difference of two solutions.

1.3
Organization of the paper

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows.

In Section
2
, we establish the Schauder-type estimates for nonlocal parabolic equations. These estimates provide the main analytical framework for treating the logarithmically critical regularity.

In Section
3
, we derive the contour-dynamics formulation of the Muskat problem. A key point is the freezing-coefficient argument, which allows us to identify the principal third-order parabolic operator and separate it from the lower-order transmission errors.

Section
4
is devoted to the estimates of the nonlinear terms. We control both the explicit critical nonlinear contribution and the implicit transmission term coming from the elliptic system.

Finally, in Section
5
, we prove the main theorem by deriving a priori estimates, constructing solutions through smooth approximation, and establishing uniqueness and stability.

This research does not have any associated data.

Note.
This manuscript corresponds to one part of a study initially published on arXiv (arXiv:2407.05313). The original comprehensive preprint has been divided into a series of papers, each separately addressing the well-posedness of certain free boundary problems, the general case of the Muskat problem, and problems with fixed boundaries. The present article forms Part II of this series.

2
Schauder-type estimates

In this section, we develop the Schauder-type estimates needed to treat the transmission operators arising in the Muskat problem. While the argument builds on the framework introduced in
[
18
]
, the estimates are adapted here to the logarithmically corrected Hölder scale and to endpoint
L^{p}
bounds, both of which play a crucial role in the critical regularity analysis below.

The main model is a nonlocal parabolic equation driven by a pseudo-differential operator of order
s>0
. Such estimates will later be applied to the frozen-coefficient operators arising from the surface-tension Muskat equation and to the elliptic transmission errors generated by the viscosity jump. In particular, the logarithmic estimates below are designed to close the nonlinear argument in the space

\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}\cap H^{1},\qquad\varkappa>1.

We first introduce the notation used throughout the paper.

•

Fourier Transform.
For a function
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we define its Fourier transform and inverse Fourier transform as

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(f)(\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)e^{-ix\cdot\xi}dx,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}(g)(x)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(\xi)e^{ix\cdot\xi}d\xi.

We also write
\hat{f}(\xi)=\mathcal{F}(f)(\xi)
for short.
It is easy to check
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{F}^{-1}(f)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{F}(f)=f
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(f\ast g)(\xi)=(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}\hat{f}(\xi)\hat{g}(\xi).

•

Finite Difference Operators.

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}f(x)=f(x)-f(x-\alpha),

\displaystyle\Delta_{\alpha}f(x)=\frac{\delta_{\alpha}f(x)}{\alpha}~~\text{in }~\mathbb{R},\quad\quad\quad\Delta_{\alpha}f(x)=\frac{\delta_{\alpha}f(x)}{|\alpha|}~~\text{in}~\mathbb{R}^{l},~l\geq 2.

The finite difference operators appearing in this paper are all in the spatial variable,
i.e.
for
h(t,x)
defined in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote
\delta_{\alpha}h(t,x)=(\delta_{\alpha}h(t,\cdot))(x)
.

•

Fractional Laplacian operators.

\Lambda=(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad\quad\quad\Lambda^{a}=(-\Delta)^{\frac{a}{2}}.

(2.1)

•

Gradient Operator.
For a vector-valued function
f=(f^{1},\dots,f^{n})\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the gradient
\nabla f
is the
d\times n
matrix given by

(\nabla f)_{ij}=\partial_{i}f^{j}.

•

Multi-Index Derivatives.

For any function
f
, and any
\beta=(\beta_{1},...\beta_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
, we denote
\partial_{x}^{\beta}f=\partial_{1}^{\beta_{1}}...\partial_{d}^{\beta_{d}}f
. With a slight abuse of notation, for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote
\nabla^{m}f=(\partial^{\beta}_{x}f)_{|\beta|=m}
, where
|\beta|=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\beta_{i}
.

•

Friedrichs Mollifier.
We denote
\rho_{\varepsilon}
the standard Friedrichs mollifier with parameter
\varepsilon>0
.

•

Integer Part.
For
s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, denote
[s]=\max\{n:n\in\mathbb{N},n\leq s\}
the integer part of
s
.

•

Time-Space Norms.
For
f:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
, we denote
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(I;X)}=\sup_{t\in I}\|f(t)\|_{X}
, where
I\subset[0,T]
and
X
is a Banach space equipped with norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}
. Specifically, when
I=[0,T]
, we simply write
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}X}
.

•

Indicator Function.
For a measurable set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, define

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{A}(x):=\begin{cases}1,\quad\quad x\in A,\\
0,\quad\quad x\notin A.\end{cases}

•

Besov Spaces.
For
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
p\in[1,\infty]
and
\kappa\in(0,1)
, we denote the
\dot{B}_{p,\infty}
norm as

\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{p,\infty}^{\kappa}}=\sup_{\alpha}\frac{\|\delta_{\alpha}f\|_{L^{p}}}{|\alpha|^{\kappa}},

and the Banach space
\dot{B}_{p,\infty}^{\kappa}
equipped with the corresponding norm. Furthermore, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote
\dot{B}_{p,\infty}^{m+\kappa}=\{f|\nabla^{m}f\in\dot{B}_{p,\infty}^{\kappa}\}
.

Consider the Cauchy problem

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u(t,x)+\mathcal{L}_{s}u(t,x)=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}f(t,x)+g(t,x)\quad\text{ in }(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},

(2.2)

\displaystyle u|_{t=0}=u_{0},

where
0<\gamma<s
are fixed parameters.

The linearized equation associated with (
1.2
) fits into the form
(
2.2
) with
s=3
, reflecting the third-order parabolic structure
generated by surface tension. We therefore formulate the Schauder estimates in
the following general pseudo-differential setting, which will later be applied
to the frozen-coefficient operators arising in the Muskat equation.

To make the assumptions precise, we impose the following conditions on the parameters, forcing terms, and principal operator:

1.
Regularity Parameters:

We fix
\varkappa>1
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, and select parameter
\kappa
such that

\displaystyle\kappa,\kappa+\gamma,\kappa+\gamma-s\notin\mathbb{N},\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ s-\gamma<\kappa<s.

(2.3)

For simplicity, denote
\kappa_{0}=\gamma-s+\kappa
.

2.
Forcing Terms:
The external input
f:(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
is a given forcing term satisfying
\mathsf{Data}_{T}(f,g)<\infty
, where

\displaystyle\mathsf{Data}_{T}(f,g)=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}

\displaystyle|\log t|^{\varkappa}(t^{\frac{\kappa}{s}}\|f(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\kappa_{0}}}+t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\|f(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{m+\kappa_{0}}})

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\|g(\tau)\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}d\tau+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\log t|^{\varkappa}t^{\frac{m}{s}+1}\|g(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{m}}.

(2.4)

For the result in
L^{p}
setting (
p\in[1,\infty]
), we suppose
\mathsf{Data}_{T}^{p}(f,g)<\infty
with

\displaystyle\mathsf{Data}_{T}^{p}(f,g)=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}(t^{\frac{\kappa}{s}}\|f(t)\|_{\dot{B}_{p,\infty}^{\kappa_{0}}}+t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\|f(t)\|_{\dot{B}_{p,\infty}^{m+\kappa_{0}}})+\|g\|_{L^{1}_{T}L^{p}}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}t^{\frac{m}{s}+1}\|\nabla^{m}g(t)\|_{L^{p}}.

(2.5)

3.
Principal Operator:
The Pseudo-differential operators
\mathcal{L}_{s}
of order
s>0
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{s}u(t,x)=(2\pi)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{A}(t,x,\xi)\hat{u}(t,\xi)e^{ix\cdot\xi}d\xi,\quad\quad\quad

(2.6)

where
\hat{u}(t,\xi)
denotes the Fourier transform of
u(t,x)
with respect to the spatial variable
x
,
\mathsf{A}(t,x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
is a matrix satisfying

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}(t,x,\xi)\geq c_{0}|\xi|^{s}\mathrm{Id},

(2.7)

\displaystyle\sum_{j,l\leq d+m+s+2}{|\xi|^{l-s}}{\left|\nabla_{x}^{j}\nabla^{l}_{\xi}\mathsf{A}(t,x,\xi)\right|}\leq c_{1},\ \ \forall\xi\neq 0,(t,x)\in(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},

for some constants
0<c_{0}<c_{1}
. Here
\mathrm{Id}
is the
N\times N
identity matrix. And
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
is a differential operator defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}f(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{B}(\xi)\hat{f}(t,\xi)e^{ix\cdot\xi}d\xi,\ \ \ \ 0<\gamma\leq s,

(2.8)

where the symbol
\mathsf{B}(\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
satisfies the growth condition

\displaystyle\left|\nabla^{j}_{\xi}\mathsf{B}(\xi)\right|\lesssim|\xi|^{\gamma-j},\ \ \ \forall 0\leq j\leq d+m+\kappa+2.

We now apply the freezing-coefficient method to (
2.2
). Fix

x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Freezing the symbol at
x_{0}
, we rewrite (
2.2
) as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u(t,x)+(2\pi)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{A}(t,x_{0},\xi)\hat{u}(t,\xi)e^{ix\cdot\xi}d\xi=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}f(t,x)+g(t,x)+R_{x_{0}}[u](t,x),

(2.9)

with

\displaystyle R_{x_{0}}[u](t,x)=(2\pi)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathsf{A}(t,x_{0},\xi)-\mathsf{A}(t,x,\xi))\hat{u}(t,\xi)e^{ix\cdot\xi}d\xi.

(2.10)

Thus the variable-coefficient equation is viewed as a constant-coefficient
equation, with the variation of the coefficients treated as a perturbative
remainder.

Let
\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,x)
denote the fundamental solution of the adjoint frozen
system

\displaystyle-\partial_{\tau}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,x)+(2\pi)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hat{\mathsf{K}}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,\xi)\mathsf{A}(\tau,x_{0},\xi)e^{ix\cdot\xi}d\xi=0,\ \ \ \ \ (\tau,x)\in(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},

(2.11)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to t^{-}}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,x)=\delta(x)\mathrm{Id},\ \ \ \ \ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

here
\mathrm{Id}
is the
N\times N
identity matrix,
\hat{\mathsf{K}}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,\xi)
is the Fourier transform of
\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,x)
in
x
-variable, and
\delta(x)
is the Dirac delta function.
By Duhamel’s formula, for every
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the solution can be written as

\displaystyle u(t,x)=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,0,x-z)u_{0}(z)dz+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,x-z)(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}f+g)(\tau,z)dzd\tau

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,x-z)R_{x_{0}}[u](\tau,z)dzd\tau

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle u_{L,x_{0}}(t,x)+u_{N,x_{0}}(t,x)+u_{R,{x_{0}}}(t,x),

(2.12)

which holds for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

We shall use the following kernel estimates for the frozen fundamental solution.
They are consequences of the ellipticity and symbol bounds in (
2.7
);
see
[
18
]
for the proof.

Lemma 2.1

The following pointwise estimates hold for
\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t_{1},t_{2},x)
:

\displaystyle\sup_{x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t_{1},t_{2},x)|\lesssim\frac{t_{1}-t_{2}}{((t_{1}-t_{2})^{\frac{1}{s}}+|x|)^{d+s}},\ \ \ \forall\ 0<t_{2}<t_{1},\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(2.13)

\displaystyle\sup_{x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\nabla_{x}^{l}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t_{1},t_{2},x)|\lesssim\frac{1}{((t_{1}-t_{2})^{\frac{1}{s}}+|x|)^{d+l}},\ \ \forall l\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.14)

And for any
\alpha\neq 0
,
0\leq\sigma<1
,

\displaystyle\left\|\sup_{x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\nabla_{x}^{l}\delta_{\alpha}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}|(t_{1},t_{2})|\cdot|^{\sigma}\right\|_{L^{1}_{x}}\lesssim|\alpha|^{\sigma}\min\left\{1,\frac{|\alpha|}{(t_{1}-t_{2})^{\frac{1}{s}}}\right\}^{1-\sigma}(t_{1}-t_{2})^{-\frac{l}{s}},\ \ \ \ \forall l\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.15)

As a direct result of (
2.13
) and (
2.14
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\sup_{x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\nabla_{x}^{l}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}|(t_{1},t_{2})|\cdot|^{\sigma}\right\|_{L^{1}_{x}}\lesssim(t_{1}-t_{2})^{-\frac{l-\sigma}{s}},\ \ \forall l\in\mathbb{N},\ 0\leq\sigma<l.

(2.16)

The implicit constants in (
2.13
)- (
2.16
) depend on
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
. Specifically, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\sup_{x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}|(t_{1},t_{2})\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq C,

(2.17)

with
C>0
that is independent of
c_{0},c_{1}
.

We prove the following Schauder-type estimate, which is an analogue to
[
18
, Proposition 2.9]
in Hölder space with logarithmic derivatives and
L^{p}
spaces. We shall need both Hölder-type and
L^{p}
-based versions of the Schauder estimate. The former is used to control the principal nonlocal evolution in the logarithmically critical topology. The latter is needed for the lower-order quantities entering through the elliptic transmission problem. Indeed, the remainder terms in the contour formulation depend implicitly on the bulk variables, and their estimates involve not only pointwise moduli of continuity but also lower-order energy norms, such as the
L^{2}
norm of the elliptic solution and of the forcing terms. For this reason we record a parallel
L^{p}
estimate, which will be used to propagate these lower-order bounds and to control the implicit forcing terms generated by the transmission system.

Lemma 2.2

i) Consider the Cauchy problem (
2.2
) with
\mathsf{A}(t,x,\xi)
satisfying (
2.7
), if
\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}+\mathsf{Data}_{T}(f,g)<\infty
, then there exists
T>0
and a unique solution

u\in C((0,T],\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{\infty}_{{loc}}((0,T],\dot{C}^{m+\kappa}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),

such that the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}(\|u(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}+|\log t|^{\varkappa}t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\|u(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{m+\kappa}})\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}+\mathsf{Data}_{T}(f,g).

(2.18)

ii) Consider the Cauchy problem (
2.2
) with
\mathsf{A}(t,x,\xi)
satisfying (
2.7
), if
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{p}}+\mathsf{Data}_{T}^{p}(f,g)<\infty
, then there exists
T>0
and a unique solution
u\in C((0,T],L^{p}_{x})\cap L^{\infty}_{{loc}}((0,T],\dot{B}^{m+\kappa}_{p,\infty})
such that the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}(\|u(t)\|_{L^{p}}+t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\|u(t)\|_{\dot{B}^{m+\kappa}_{p,\infty}})\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{L^{p}}+\mathsf{Data}_{T}^{p}(f,g).

(2.19)

Proof.
We only establish a priori estimates. The existence and uniqueness of solution can be obtained following
[
18
]
. Let
T\in(0,1)
. Denote

\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}}=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}(\|u(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}+|\log t|^{\varkappa}t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\|u(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{m+\kappa}}),

(2.20)

\|u\|_{Y_{T}^{p}}=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\|u(t)\|_{L^{p}}+t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\|u(t)\|_{\dot{B}_{p,\infty}^{m+\kappa}}\right).

By interpolation, for every
t\in(0,T)
, we obtain that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\sigma\in(0,1)
such that
n+\sigma\leq m+\kappa
,

\displaystyle\|u(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{n+\sigma}}\lesssim|\log t|^{-\varkappa}t^{-\frac{n+\sigma}{s}}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}},

(2.21)

\displaystyle\|u(t)\|_{\dot{B}^{n+\sigma}_{p,\infty}}\lesssim|\log t|^{-\varkappa}t^{-\frac{n+\sigma}{s}}\|u\|_{Y_{T}^{p}}.

By the formula (
2.12
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{n}u\|_{L^{p}}\leq\|(\nabla^{n}u_{L,x_{0}})|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{p}}+\|(\nabla^{n}u_{N,x_{0}})|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{p}}+\|(\nabla^{n}u_{R,x_{0}})|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{p}},

(2.22)

\displaystyle\|\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{n}u\|_{L^{p}}\leq\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{n}u_{L,x_{0}})|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{p}}+\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{n}u_{N,x_{0}})|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{p}}+\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{n}u_{R,x_{0}})|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{p}},

for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Step 1. Estimate of
u_{L,x_{0}}

We first consider

u_{L,x_{0}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,0,z)u_{0}(x-z)dz.

By (
2.16
), it holds

\|u_{L,x_{0}}(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}\lesssim\big\|\sup_{z}|\mathsf{K}_{z}(t,0,\cdot)|\big\|_{L^{1}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{L^{p}},

(2.23)

and

\displaystyle\|(\delta_{\alpha}u_{L,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\mathsf{K}_{z}(t,0,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}\|\delta_{\alpha}u_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\log^{-\varkappa}\left(2+|\alpha|^{-1}\right)\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}.

(2.24)

Moreover,
we have

\displaystyle|\delta_{\alpha}\nabla_{x}^{m+[\kappa]}u_{L,x_{0}}(t,x)|=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\delta_{\alpha}\nabla_{x}^{m+[\kappa]}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,0,z)u_{0}(x-z)dz\right|.

Fix
x_{0}=x
, and take
L^{p}
in
x
on both sides, from (
2.15
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}u_{L,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\sup_{z}|\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}\mathsf{K}_{z}|(t,0,\cdot)\right\|_{L^{1}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{p}}

(2.25)

\displaystyle\lesssim|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}t^{-\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{p}}.

On the other hand, observe that
\int\delta_{\alpha}\nabla_{x}^{m+[\kappa]}K_{x_{0}}(t,0,z)dz=0
, we obtain

\displaystyle|\delta_{\alpha}\nabla_{x}^{m+[\kappa]}u_{L,x_{0}}(t,x)|=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\delta_{\alpha}\nabla_{x}^{m+[\kappa]}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,0,z)||u_{0}(x-z)-u_{0}(x)|dz

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\delta_{\alpha}\nabla_{z}^{m+[\kappa]}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,0,z)|\log^{-\varkappa}\left(2+|z|^{-1}\right)dz\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}.

Using the elementary inequality

|\log(b)|\lesssim\log(2+a^{-1})\log(2+ab),\ \ \ \forall a>0,b>2,

(2.26)

we obtain

\log^{-\varkappa}\left(2+|z|^{-1}\right)\lesssim\log^{\varkappa}\left(2+(t^{-\frac{1}{s}}|z|)\right)|\log t|^{-\varkappa}.

Observe that
\log^{\varkappa}(2+(t^{-\frac{1}{s}}|x|))\lesssim 1+(t^{-\frac{1}{s}}|x|)^{\eta}
for
0<\eta\ll 1
. Combining this with (
2.15
) yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\delta_{\alpha}\nabla_{z}^{m+[\kappa]}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,0,z)|\log^{-\varkappa}\left(2+|z|^{-1}\right)dz

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\lesssim|\log t|^{-\varkappa}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\delta_{\alpha}\nabla_{z}^{m+[\kappa]}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,0,z)|(1+(t^{-\frac{1}{s}}|z|)^{\eta})dz

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\lesssim|\log t|^{-\varkappa}t^{-\frac{m+[\kappa]+\eta}{s}}|\alpha|^{\eta}\min\{1,t^{-\frac{1}{s}}|\alpha|\}^{1-\eta}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\lesssim|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}|\log t|^{-\varkappa}t^{-\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}.

So we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha}\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla_{x}^{m+[\kappa]}u_{L,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}}\lesssim|\log t|^{-\varkappa}t^{-\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}.

(2.27)

We conclude from (
2.23
) and (
2.25
) that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\|u_{L,x_{0}}(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}+t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\sup_{\alpha}\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}u_{L,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}}{|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}}\right)\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{L^{p}},

(2.28)

and from (
2.24
), (
2.27
) that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}u_{L,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{\log^{-\varkappa}(2+|\alpha|^{-1})}+|\log t|^{\varkappa}t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla_{x}^{m+[\kappa]}u_{L,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}}\right)

(2.29)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}.

Step 2. Estimate of
u_{N,x_{0}}

Then we consider
u_{N,x_{0}}=u_{N,1,x_{0}}+u_{N,2,x_{0}}
with

\displaystyle u_{N,1,x_{0}}(t,x)=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,x-z)\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}f(\tau,z)dzd\tau,

(2.30)

\displaystyle u_{N,2,x_{0}}(t,x)=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,x-z)g(\tau,z)dzd\tau.

(2.31)

First, for
u_{N,1,x_{0}}
,
recalling the definition of
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
in (
2.8
), we have

\displaystyle u_{N,1,x_{0}}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}L_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,x-z)\Lambda^{\vartheta}f(\tau,z)dzd\tau.

Here
L_{x_{0}}(t,x)
is defined by

L_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,x)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\mathsf{B}(\xi)}{|\xi|^{\vartheta}}\mathcal{F}(\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau))(\xi)e^{ix\cdot\xi}d\xi,

for some
\vartheta\in((\kappa_{0}-1)_{+},\kappa_{0})
.

Since the Fourier multiplier satisfies

\left.\frac{\mathsf{B}(\xi)}{|\xi|^{\vartheta}}\widehat{\mathsf{K}}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,\xi)\right|_{\xi=0}=0,

we have

\widehat{L}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,0)=0.

Therefore,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}L_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,x)\,dx=0.

By the pointwise estimate Lemma 2.1 in
[
18
]
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{z}|\nabla^{k}L_{z}(t,\tau,y)|\lesssim(t-\tau)^{-\frac{d+k+\gamma-\vartheta}{s}}\left(1+\frac{|y|}{(t-\tau)^{\frac{1}{s}}}\right)^{-d-\gamma+\vartheta},

(2.32)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{z}|\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{k}L_{z}(t,\tau,y)||y|^{\sigma}dy\lesssim|\alpha|^{\sigma}(t-\tau)^{-\frac{k+\sigma+\gamma-\vartheta}{s}}\left\{1,\frac{|\alpha|}{(t-\tau)^{\frac{1}{s}}}\right\}^{1-\sigma},

for any
\sigma\in(0,\gamma-\vartheta)
. Then, we obtain

\displaystyle\|u_{N,1,x_{0}}(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{p}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{z}|L_{z}(t,\tau,y)|\|\Lambda^{\vartheta}f(\tau,x-y)-\Lambda^{\vartheta}f(\tau,x)\|_{L^{p}_{x}}dyd\tau

(2.33)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{z}|L_{z}(t,\tau,y)||y|^{\kappa_{0}-\vartheta}dy\|\Lambda^{\vartheta}f(\tau)\|_{\dot{B}^{\kappa_{0}-\vartheta}_{p,\infty}}d\tau

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{S2eq32}}{\lesssim}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{(t-\tau)^{\frac{\gamma-\kappa_{0}}{s}}\tau^{\frac{\kappa}{s}}}d\tau\sup_{t\in[0,T]}t^{\frac{\kappa}{s}}\|f(t)\|_{\dot{B}^{\kappa_{0}}_{p,\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathsf{Data}_{T}^{p}(f,0),

where in the third inequality we used the standard equivalence

\|\Lambda^{\vartheta}f(\tau)\|_{\dot{B}^{\kappa_{0}-\vartheta}_{p,\infty}}\sim\|f(\tau)\|_{\dot{B}^{\kappa_{0}}_{p,\infty}}.

Moreover, for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle|\delta_{\alpha}u_{N,1,x_{0}}(t,x)|\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\delta_{\alpha}L_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,x-z)(\Lambda^{\vartheta}f(\tau,z)-\Lambda^{\vartheta}f(\tau,x))d\tau dz\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\delta_{\alpha}L_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,z)||z|^{\kappa_{0}-\vartheta}dz\|f(\tau)\|_{\dot{C}^{\kappa_{0}}}d\tau

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{S2eq32}}{\lesssim}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{|\alpha|^{\kappa_{0}-\vartheta}}{(t-\tau)^{\frac{\gamma-\vartheta}{s}}|\log\tau|^{\varkappa}\tau^{\frac{\kappa}{s}}}\min\left\{1,\frac{|\alpha|}{(t-\tau)^{\frac{1}{s}}}\right\}^{1-(\kappa_{0}-\vartheta)}d\tau\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\log t|^{\varkappa}t^{\frac{\kappa}{s}}\|f(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\kappa_{0}}}.

Note that
|\log t|\lesssim|\log\tau|
for any
0<\tau<t<\frac{1}{2}
, then it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{|\alpha|^{\kappa_{0}-\vartheta}}{(t-\tau)^{\frac{\gamma-\vartheta}{s}}|\log\tau|^{\varkappa}\tau^{\frac{\kappa}{s}}}\min\left\{1,\frac{|\alpha|}{(t-\tau)^{\frac{1}{s}}}\right\}^{1-(\kappa_{0}-\vartheta)}d\tau

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\lesssim|\log t|^{-\varkappa}\min\left\{1,|\alpha|t^{-1/s}\right\}^{\varepsilon}\lesssim\log^{-\varkappa}(2+|\alpha|^{-1}),

where
0<\varepsilon<\min\{1,\frac{\kappa}{10}\}
. The last inequality can be proved by considering the case
|\alpha|<t^{\frac{1}{s}}
and
|\alpha|>t^{\frac{1}{s}}
separately. If
|\alpha|>t^{\frac{1}{s}}
, it holds automatically. Otherwise denote
b=|\alpha|t^{-\frac{1}{s}}<1
. If
b<t^{\frac{1}{2s}}
, then
b^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\kappa}}\log(2+b^{-1}t^{-\frac{1}{s}})\lesssim b^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\kappa}}\log(2+b^{-\frac{3}{2}})\lesssim 1
. If
b>t^{\frac{1}{2s}}
, then
b^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\kappa}}\log(2+b^{-1}t^{-\frac{1}{s}})\lesssim\log(2+t^{-\frac{1}{2s}})\lesssim|\log t|
.

Hence, we deduce that

\displaystyle\log^{\varkappa}(2+|\alpha|^{-1})\|(\delta_{\alpha}u_{N,1,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\mathsf{Data}_{T}(f,0).

(2.34)

For higher order derivatives, using integration by parts, we obtain

\displaystyle\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}u_{N,1,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{p}_{x}}

(2.35)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{\frac{t}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{z}|\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}L_{z}(t,\tau,y)|\|\Lambda^{\vartheta}f(\tau,x-y)-\Lambda^{\vartheta}f(\tau,x)\|_{L^{p}_{x}}dyd\tau

\displaystyle+\int_{\frac{t}{2}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{z}|\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{[\kappa]}L_{z}(t,\tau,y)|\|\Lambda^{\vartheta}\nabla^{m}f(\tau,x-y)-\Lambda^{\vartheta}\nabla^{m}f(\tau,x)\|_{L^{p}_{x}}dyd\tau

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{S2eq32}}{\lesssim}|\alpha|^{-\vartheta+\kappa_{0}}\int_{0}^{\frac{t}{2}}\frac{\min\{1,\frac{|\alpha|}{(t-\tau)^{\frac{1}{s}}}\}^{1-\kappa_{0}+\vartheta}}{(t-\tau)^{\frac{m+\gamma+\vartheta-\kappa_{0}}{s}}\tau^{\frac{\kappa}{s}}}d\tau\sup_{t\in[0,T]}t^{\frac{\kappa}{s}}\|f(t)\|_{\dot{B}^{\kappa_{0}}_{p,\infty}}

\displaystyle+|\alpha|^{-\vartheta+\kappa_{0}}\int_{\frac{t}{2}}^{t}\frac{\min\{1,\frac{|\alpha|}{(t-\tau)^{\frac{1}{s}}}\}^{1-\kappa_{0}+\vartheta}}{(t-\tau)^{\frac{\gamma+\vartheta-\kappa_{0}}{s}}\tau^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}}d\tau\sup_{t\in[0,T]}t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\|f(t)\|_{\dot{B}^{m+\kappa_{0}}_{p,\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}t^{-\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\mathsf{Data}_{T}^{p}(f,0).

Specifically, in the case
p=\infty
, we also have

\displaystyle\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}u_{N,1,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

(2.36)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{S2eq32}}{\lesssim}|\alpha|^{-\vartheta+\kappa_{0}}\int_{0}^{\frac{t}{2}}\frac{\min\{1,\frac{|\alpha|}{(t-\tau)^{\frac{1}{s}}}\}^{1-\kappa_{0}+\vartheta}}{(t-\tau)^{\frac{m+\gamma+\vartheta-\kappa_{0}}{s}}\tau^{\frac{\kappa}{s}}|\log\tau|^{\varkappa}}d\tau\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\log t|^{\varkappa}t^{\frac{\kappa}{s}}\|f(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\kappa_{0}}}

\displaystyle+|\alpha|^{-\vartheta+\kappa_{0}}\int_{\frac{t}{2}}^{t}\frac{\min\{1,\frac{|\alpha|}{(t-\tau)^{\frac{1}{s}}}\}^{1-\kappa_{0}+\vartheta}}{(t-\tau)^{\frac{\gamma+\vartheta-\kappa_{0}}{s}}\tau^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}|\log\tau|^{\varkappa}}d\tau\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\log t|^{\varkappa}t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\|f(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{m+\kappa_{0}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}|\log t|^{-\varkappa}t^{-\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\mathsf{Data}_{T}(f,0).

We conclude from (
2.33
), (
2.34
), (
2.35
) and (
2.36
) that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\|u_{N,1,x_{0}}(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}+t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\sup_{\alpha}\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}u_{N,1,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}}{|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}}\right)\lesssim\mathsf{Data}_{T}^{p}(f,0),

(2.37)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}u_{N,1,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{\log^{-\varkappa}(2+|\alpha|^{-1})}+|\log t|^{\varkappa}t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla_{x}^{m+[\kappa]}u_{N,1,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}}\right)

(2.38)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathsf{Data}_{T}(f,0).

For
u_{N,2,x_{0}}
, by (
2.31
) and Lemma
2.1
we have

\displaystyle\|u_{N,2,x_{0}}(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{p}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\big\|\sup_{z}|\mathsf{K}_{z}(t,\tau)|\big\|_{L^{1}}\|g(\tau)\|_{L^{p}}d\tau\lesssim\mathsf{Data}_{T}^{p}(0,g),

(2.39)

\displaystyle\|(\delta_{\alpha}u_{N,2,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\log^{-\varkappa}(2+|\alpha|^{-1})\int_{0}^{t}\big\|\sup_{z}|\mathsf{K}_{z}(t,\tau)|\big\|_{L^{1}}\|g(\tau)\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}d\tau

\displaystyle\lesssim\log^{-\varkappa}(2+|\alpha|^{-1})\mathsf{Data}_{T}(0,g).

For higher order derivative, using integration by parts and applying (
2.15
),

\displaystyle\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}u_{N,2,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{\frac{t}{2}}\big\|\sup_{z}|\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}\mathsf{K}_{z}|(t,\tau)\big\|_{L^{1}}\|g(\tau)\|_{L^{p}}d\tau

(2.40)

\displaystyle+\int_{\frac{t}{2}}^{t}\big\|\sup_{z}|\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{[\kappa]}\mathsf{K}_{z}|(t,\tau)\big\|_{L^{1}}\|\nabla^{m}g(\tau)\|_{L^{p}}d\tau

\displaystyle\lesssim|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}\left(t^{-\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\|g\|_{L_{T}^{1}L^{p}}+\int_{\frac{t}{2}}^{t}(t-\tau)^{-\frac{\kappa}{s}}\|\nabla^{m}g(\tau)\|_{L^{p}}d\tau\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}t^{-\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\mathsf{Data}_{T}^{p}(0,g).

Similarly, we obtain

\displaystyle\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}u_{N,2,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}\lesssim|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}|\log t|^{-\varkappa}t^{-\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\mathsf{Data}_{T}^{p}(0,g).

(2.41)

Combining (
2.37
)–(
2.41
) yields

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{\alpha}\left(\frac{{\|(\delta_{\alpha}u_{N,x_{0}})|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}}}}{\log^{-\varkappa}(2+|\alpha|^{-1})}+|\log t|^{\varkappa}t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}u_{N,x_{0}})|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}}\right)\lesssim\mathsf{Data}_{T}(f,g),

(2.42)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\|u_{N,x_{0}}(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}+\sup_{\alpha}t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}u_{N,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}}{|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}}\right)\lesssim\mathsf{Data}_{T}^{p}(f,g).

(2.43)

Step 3. Estimate of
u_{R,x_{0}}

Finally, we estimate the remainder term
u_{R,x_{0}}
. Let
0<\sigma<s
. Denote

\displaystyle R^{1}_{x_{0}}[u](t,x)=(2\pi)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\xi|^{-\sigma}(\mathsf{A}(t,x_{0},\xi)-\mathsf{A}(t,x,\xi))\widehat{u}(t,\xi)e^{ix\cdot\xi}d\xi,

\displaystyle R^{2}[u](t,x)=C_{d,\sigma}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\xi|^{-\sigma}(\mathsf{A}(t,x-z,\xi)-\mathsf{A}(t,x,\xi))\widehat{\tau_{z}u}(t,\xi)e^{ix\cdot\xi}\frac{d\xi dz}{|z|^{d+\sigma}},

with
\tau_{z}u(x)=u(x+z)
. Then

\displaystyle u_{R,{x_{0}}}(t,x)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Lambda^{\sigma}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,x-z)R_{x_{0}}^{1}[u](\tau,z)dzd\tau-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,x-z)R^{2}[u](\tau,z)dzd\tau

\displaystyle:=u_{R,{x_{0}}}^{1}(t,x)+u_{R,{x_{0}}}^{2}(t,x).

(2.44)

We need the following technical lemma, the proof of which can be derived from singular integral theory.

Lemma 2.3

Let
\mathbf{m}(x,\xi)
be a Fourier multiplier of order
\sigma>-d
, with

\displaystyle\sup_{x}|\nabla_{\xi}^{k}\mathbf{m}(x,\xi)|\leq|\xi|^{\sigma-k},\ \ \ \forall 0\leq k\leq d+|\sigma|+1.

Define

\mathcal{T}f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbf{m}(x,\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi)e^{ix\cdot\xi}\,d\xi.

Then, for every
1<p<\infty
and every sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
,

\|\mathcal{T}f\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\|\Lambda^{\sigma+\varepsilon}f\|_{L^{p}}^{1/2}\|\Lambda^{\sigma-\varepsilon}f\|_{L^{p}}^{1/2}.

We now return to the estimates of
R_{x_{0}}^{1}[u]
and
R_{x_{0}}^{2}[u]
. By Lemma
2.3
and (
2.21
), for any

\eta^{\prime}\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle|R_{x_{0}}^{1}[u](t,x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim|x_{0}-x|^{\eta^{\prime}}\|u\|_{\dot{C}^{s-\sigma-\varepsilon}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u\|_{\dot{C}^{s-\sigma+\varepsilon}}^{\frac{1}{2}}

(2.45)

\displaystyle\lesssim|x_{0}-x|^{\eta^{\prime}}|\log t|^{-\varkappa}t^{-\frac{s-\sigma}{s}}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}}.

The estimate for higher derivatives follows in the same way, except that
derivatives may also fall on the
x
-dependent symbol. More precisely, when

\nabla_{x}^{k}
is applied to
R_{x_{0}}^{1}[u]
, we obtain terms of the form

(2\pi)^{-\frac{d}{2}}i^{k_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\xi|^{-\sigma}\nabla_{x}^{k_{1}}(\mathsf{A}(t,x_{0},\xi)-\mathsf{A}(t,x,\xi))\,\widehat{u}(t,\xi)\,\xi^{\otimes k_{2}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}\,d\xi,\qquad k_{1}+k_{2}=k.

If
k_{1}=0
, the previous argument gives the factor

|x_{0}-x|^{\eta^{\prime}}|\log t|^{-\varkappa}t^{-\frac{k+s-\sigma}{s}}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}}.

If
k_{1}>0
, the derivative falls on the coefficient. In this case we use
Lemma
2.3
with a symbol of order
k_{2}+s-\sigma
. This yields

\left|(2\pi)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\xi|^{-\sigma}\nabla_{x}^{k_{1}}\mathsf{A}(t,x,\xi)\widehat{u}(t,\xi)\xi^{\otimes k_{2}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}\,d\xi\right|\lesssim t^{1/s}|\log t|^{-\varkappa}t^{-\frac{k+s-\sigma}{s}}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}}.

Therefore,

|\nabla_{x}^{k}R_{x_{0}}^{1}[u](t,x)|\lesssim\left(|x_{0}-x|^{\eta^{\prime}}+t^{1/s}\right)|\log t|^{-\varkappa}t^{-\frac{k+s-\sigma}{s}}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}}.

(2.46)

For
R^{2}[u]
, we apply Lemma
2.3
with

\mathbf{m}_{z}(x,\xi)=|\xi|^{-\sigma}\frac{\mathsf{A}(t,x-z,\xi)-\mathsf{A}(t,x,\xi)}{\min\{1,|z|\}},

for each fixed
z\neq 0
. Then applying (
2.21
) yields

\displaystyle\|R^{2}[u](t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\min\{1,z\}}{|z|^{d+\sigma}}dz\|\Lambda^{s-\sigma+\epsilon}u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\Lambda^{s-\sigma-\epsilon}u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim t^{-\frac{s-\sigma}{s}}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}}.

Here we used
\sigma<1
, so that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\min\{1,|z|\}}{|z|^{d+\sigma}}\,dz<\infty.

Similarly, we can prove for any
0\leq k\leq m
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{k}R^{2}[u](t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim t^{-\frac{k+s-\sigma}{s}}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}}.

(2.47)

Combining (
2.45
), (
2.46
), and Lemma
2.1
, we first estimate the low-order part of
u_{R,x_{0}}^{1}
. Taking
x_{0}=x
, we obtain

\displaystyle|u_{R,x_{0}}^{1}(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\ \int_{0}^{t}|\log\tau|^{-\varkappa}\tau^{-\frac{s-\sigma}{s}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{z}|\Lambda^{\sigma}\mathsf{K}_{z}(t,\tau,x-y)||x-y|^{\eta^{\prime}}dyd\tau\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}(t-\tau)^{-\frac{\sigma-\eta^{\prime}}{s}}|\log\tau|^{-\varkappa}\tau^{-\frac{s-\sigma}{s}}d\tau\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}}\lesssim|\log t|^{-\varkappa}T^{\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{s}}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}},

\displaystyle|\nabla u_{R,x_{0}}^{1}(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}|\log\tau|^{-\varkappa}\tau^{-\frac{s-\sigma}{s}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{z}|\nabla\Lambda^{\sigma}\mathsf{K}_{z}(t,\tau,x-y)||x-y|dyd\tau\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}(t-\tau)^{-\frac{\sigma}{s}}|\log\tau|^{-\varkappa}\tau^{-\frac{s-\sigma}{s}}d\tau\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}}\lesssim|\log t|^{-\varkappa}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}},

for any
0<\eta^{\prime}<\sigma
.

Interpolating between these two bounds yields, for some
\sigma^{\prime}\in(0,\sigma)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha}\left(\|(\delta_{\alpha}u_{R,x_{0}}^{1})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}}\log^{\varkappa}(2+|\alpha|^{-1})\right)\lesssim T^{\frac{\sigma^{\prime}}{s}}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}}.

We estimate higher order derivatives. By taking
\sigma\in(s-\kappa,s-[\kappa])
, and decomposing the integral interval into
[0,\frac{t}{2}]\cup[\frac{t}{2},t]
, we obtain

\displaystyle|\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}u_{R,x_{0}}^{1}(t,x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|\int_{0}^{\frac{t}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}\Lambda_{z}^{\sigma}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,z)R_{x_{0}}^{1}(\tau,x-z)dzd\tau\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\left|\int_{\frac{t}{2}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{[\kappa]}\Lambda_{z}^{\sigma}\mathsf{K}_{x_{0}}(t,\tau,z)\nabla^{m}R_{x_{0}}^{1}(\tau,x-z)dzd\tau\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim(|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}+|x_{0}-x|^{\kappa-[\kappa]})T^{\frac{\kappa-[\kappa]}{s}}|\log t|^{-\varkappa}t^{-\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}}.

So we have proved

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{\alpha}\left(\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}u_{R,x_{0}}^{1})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\log^{-\varkappa}(2+|\alpha|^{-1})}+|\log t|^{\varkappa}t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}u_{R,x_{0}}^{1})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\lesssim T^{\frac{\sigma^{\prime}}{s}}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}},

(2.48)

for some
\sigma^{\prime}\in(0,\sigma)
.
For the corresponding
L^{p}
estimates of
u^{1}_{R,x_{0}}
, note that by Lemma
2.3
and similarly to (
2.45
), we have

\displaystyle\|R_{x_{0}}^{1}[u](t,x-z)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}\lesssim|z|^{\sigma^{\prime}}t^{-\frac{s-\sigma}{s}}\|u\|_{Y_{T}^{p}},

(2.49)

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{k}R_{x_{0}}^{1}[u](t,x-z)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}\lesssim\left(|z|^{\sigma^{\prime}}+t^{\frac{1}{s}}\right)t^{-\frac{s-\sigma}{s}}\|u\|_{Y_{T}^{p}},\quad\forall 0\leq k\leq m,

for some
\sigma^{\prime}\in(0,\sigma)
, which leads to

\displaystyle\|u_{R,x_{0}}^{1}(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\|\sup_{z}|\Lambda^{\sigma}\mathsf{K}_{z}|(t,\tau,y)|y|^{\eta^{\prime}}\|_{L_{y}^{1}}\||y|^{-\eta^{\prime}}(R_{x_{0}}^{1}[u])(\tau,x-y)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{z}^{\infty}L_{x}^{p}}d\tau

\displaystyle\lesssim T^{\frac{\sigma^{\prime}}{s}}\|u\|_{Y_{T}^{p}}.

Similarly, for the estimate of
\dot{B}_{p,\infty}^{m+\kappa}
norm, by (
2.49
), we have

\displaystyle\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}u_{R,x_{0}}^{1})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{\frac{t}{2}}\left\|\sup_{z}|\delta_{\alpha}\nabla_{z}^{m+[\kappa]}\Lambda^{\sigma}\mathsf{K}_{z}|(t,\tau,y)|y|^{\eta^{\prime}}\right\|_{L_{y}^{1}}\left\||y|^{-\eta^{\prime}}(R_{x_{0}}^{1}[u])|_{x_{0}=x}(\tau,x-y)\right\|_{L_{y}^{\infty}L_{x}^{p}}d\tau

\displaystyle+\int_{\frac{t}{2}}^{t}\left\|\sup_{z}|\delta_{\alpha}\Lambda^{\sigma}\nabla^{[\kappa]}\mathsf{K}_{z}|(t,\tau,y)(\nabla_{z}^{m}R_{x_{0}}^{1}[u])|_{x_{0}=x}(\tau,x-y)\right\|_{L_{y}^{1}L_{x}^{p}}d\tau

\displaystyle\lesssim|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}t^{-\frac{m+\kappa-\sigma^{\prime}}{s}}\|u\|_{Y_{T}^{p}}.

So we have proved

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\|u_{R,x_{0}}^{1}(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}+t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}u_{R,x_{0}}^{1})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}}{|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}}\right)\lesssim T^{\frac{\sigma^{\prime}}{s}}\|u\|_{Y_{T}^{p}},

(2.50)

for some
\sigma^{\prime}\in(0,\sigma)
. The estimates for
u_{R,x_{0}}^{2}
follow similarly from the
u_{N,2,x_{0}}
part of (
2.42
) and (
2.47
), say,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{\alpha}\left(\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}u_{R,x_{0}}^{2})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\log^{-\varkappa}(2+|\alpha|^{-1})}+|\log t|^{\varkappa}t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}u_{R,x_{0}}^{2})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\|R_{2}[u](\tau)\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}d\tau+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\log t|^{\varkappa}t^{\frac{m}{s}+1}\|R_{2}[u](t)\|_{\dot{C}^{m}}\lesssim T^{\frac{\sigma}{s}}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}}.

(2.51)

Similarly, by (
2.39
), (
2.40
) and Lemma
2.3
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\|(u_{R,x_{0}}^{2}(t,x))|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}+t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}u_{R,x_{0}}^{2})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}}{|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\|R_{2}[u](\tau)\|_{L^{p}}d\tau+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}t^{\frac{m}{s}+1}\|R_{2}[u](t)\|_{W^{m,p}}\lesssim T^{\frac{\sigma}{s}}\|u\|_{Y_{T}^{p}}.

(2.52)

We obtain from (
2
) and (
2
) that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}u_{R,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{\log^{-\varkappa}(2+|\alpha|^{-1})}+|\log t|^{\varkappa}t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla_{x}^{m+[\kappa]}u_{R,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}}\right)

(2.53)

\displaystyle\lesssim T^{\frac{\sigma^{\prime}}{s}}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}},

and from (
2.50
) and (
2
) that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\|u_{R,x_{0}}(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}+t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{s}}\sup_{\alpha}\frac{\|(\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{m+[\kappa]}u_{R,x_{0}})(t,x)|_{x_{0}=x}\|_{L_{x}^{p}}}{|\alpha|^{\kappa-[\kappa]}}\right)\lesssim T^{\frac{\sigma^{\prime}}{s}}\|u\|_{Y_{T}^{p}},

(2.54)

for some
\sigma^{\prime}\in(0,\sigma)
. Combining (
2.29
), (
2.42
) and (
2.53
) yields

\displaystyle\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}+\mathsf{Data}_{T}(f,g)+T^{\frac{\sigma^{\prime}}{s}}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{\varkappa}},

and (
2.28
), (
2.43
), (
2.54
) give

\|u\|_{Y_{T}^{p}}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{L^{p}}+\mathsf{Data}_{T}^{p}(f,g)+T^{\frac{\sigma^{\prime}}{s}}\|u\|_{Y_{T}^{p}}.

These imply (
2.18
) and (
2.19
) by taking
T
small enough.

3
Contour dynamics formulation

The purpose of this section is to extract the leading singular part of the interface equation. This is one of the main contributions of the paper. In the general two-phase problem with viscosity jump and rigid boundaries, the interface velocity is not given by a closed explicit contour equation. Instead, it is obtained through the elliptic transmission problem (
1.1
). We show that, nevertheless, the evolution contains an explicit third-order parabolic principal operator generated by surface tension, while all effects coming from the viscosity jump, density contrast, and rigid boundaries can be placed into lower-order or perturbative terms.

The key point is to compare the true transmission velocity with a frozen-coefficient velocity obtained by freezing the geometry at the local slope of the interface. This allows us to identify the leading order operator in a singular-integral form. The resulting formula (
3.4
) is the starting point for the Schauder estimates and the nonlinear well-posedness argument in the logarithmically critical space.

Under the uniform separation condition from the fixed boundaries, the boundary contributions from
\Gamma^{\pm}
do not affect the leading singular structure. Similarly, the density contrast term
\varrho_{0}\eta
is lower order compared with the surface-tension contribution

-\operatorname{div}\left(\frac{\nabla\eta}{\langle\nabla\eta\rangle}\right).

We therefore first straighten the moving interface and then freeze the coefficients of the resulting elliptic transmission problem. More precisely,
define

\displaystyle v^{\pm}(x,z)=v^{\pm}_{\eta}(x,z)=q^{\pm}(x,z+\eta(x)).

(3.1)

Then

\displaystyle\nabla_{x,z}q^{\pm}(x,z)=\mathsf{M}_{0}(\nabla\eta(x))(\nabla_{x,z}v^{\pm})(x,z-\eta(x)),

\displaystyle\Delta_{x,z}q^{\pm}(x,z)=\left(\operatorname{div}_{x,z}(\mathsf{M}(\nabla\eta(x))\nabla_{x,z}v^{\pm})\right)(x,z-\eta(x)),

with

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}_{0}(\nabla\eta)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{Id}&-\nabla\eta\\
0&1\end{array}\right),

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}(\nabla\eta)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{M}_{0}(\nabla\eta))^{\top}\mathsf{M}_{0}(\nabla\eta)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{Id}&-\nabla\eta\\
-(\nabla\eta)^{\top}&1+|\nabla\eta|^{2}\end{array}\right).

We obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}_{x,z}(\mathsf{M}(\nabla\eta)\nabla_{x,z}v^{\pm})=0,\ \ \text{in}\ \tilde{\Omega}_{\eta}^{\pm},

(3.3)

\displaystyle v^{-}-v^{+}=-\operatorname{div}\left(\frac{\nabla\eta}{\langle\nabla\eta\rangle}\right)+\varrho_{0}\eta,\quad\text{ on }\{z=0\},

\displaystyle e_{d+1}\cdot(\mathsf{M}(\nabla\eta)(\mu_{+}^{-1}\nabla_{x,z}v^{+}-\mu_{-}^{-1}\nabla_{x,z}v^{-}))=0,\quad\text{ on }\{z=0\},

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}^{\pm}\cdot(\mathsf{M}(\nabla\eta)\nabla_{x,z}v^{\pm})=0,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{on}\ \tilde{\Gamma}^{\pm}_{\eta},

where
e_{d+1}
denotes the (d+1)-th unit vector,

\displaystyle\tilde{\Omega}_{\eta}^{+}=\left\{(x,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}:0<z<\underline{b}^{+}(x)-\eta(t,x)\right\},

(3.4)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Omega}_{\eta}^{-}=\left\{(x,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}:\underline{b}^{-}(x)-\eta(t,x)<z<0\right\},

\displaystyle\tilde{\Gamma}^{\pm}_{\eta}=\{(x,z):z=\underline{b}^{\pm}(x)-\eta(t,x)\},

and
\tilde{\nu}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{\langle\nabla\left(\underline{b}^{\pm}(x)-\eta(t,x)\right)\rangle}\left(-\nabla\underline{b}^{\pm}(x)+\nabla\eta(t,x),1\right)
denote the outward normal vector of
\tilde{\Gamma}_{\eta}^{\pm}
.
Note that we transform the effect of free interface
\Sigma_{t}=\{(x,z):z=\eta(x)\}
in the system (
1.1
) to the variable coefficient
\mathsf{M}(\nabla\eta)
in the system (
3.3
).
We remark that
v^{\pm}
is not differentiable at
z=0
. When evaluating a function with a “
+
” or “
-
” superscript at
z=0
, it implies approaching the limit from either the positive or negative direction.
i.e.

v^{\pm}(x,0)=\lim_{z=0^{\pm}}v^{\pm}(x,z)
.

With this notation, the interface equation (
1.2
) becomes

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\eta(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\mu_{+}}e_{d+1}\cdot(\mathsf{M}(\nabla\eta)\nabla_{x,z}v^{+}(x,z)|_{z=0})

(3.5)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu_{+}}\nabla\eta(x)\cdot\nabla_{x}v^{+}(x,z)|_{z=0}-\frac{1}{\mu_{+}}\langle\nabla\eta(x)\rangle^{2}\partial_{z}v^{+}(x,z)|_{z=0},

\displaystyle\eta|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\eta_{0}.

Here and throughout, traces at
z=0
are understood from the corresponding side of the interface. The formulation (
3.5
) is well-defined by the existence and uniqueness of weak solutions to the elliptic transmission problem (
3.3
).

Remark 3.1

The weak solvability of (
3.3
) follows from a standard variational
argument. Indeed, after removing the prescribed jump

g_{\eta}:=-\operatorname{div}\left(\frac{\nabla\eta}{\langle\nabla\eta\rangle}\right)+\varrho_{0}\eta

across
\{z=0\}
, the transmission system can be written as a divergence-form
elliptic equation with piecewise coefficients and a source supported on the flat
interface.

More precisely, let
v_{0}^{+}
solve

\begin{cases}\operatorname{div}_{x,z}\bigl(\mathsf{M}(\nabla\eta)\nabla_{x,z}v_{0}^{+}\bigr)=0,&\text{in }\tilde{\Omega}_{\eta}^{+},\\
v_{0}^{+}=g_{\eta},&\text{on }\{z=0\},\\
\widetilde{\nu}^{+}\cdot\mathsf{M}(\nabla\eta)\nabla_{x,z}v_{0}^{+}=0,&\text{on }\tilde{\Gamma}_{\eta}^{+}.\end{cases}

Define

u^{+}:=v^{+}+v_{0}^{+},\qquad u^{-}:=v^{-}.

Since
v^{-}-v^{+}=g_{\eta}
on
\{z=0\}
, we have

u^{+}=u^{-}\qquad\text{on }\{z=0\}.

Thus the function
u
, defined by

u=\begin{cases}u^{+},&\text{in }\tilde{\Omega}_{\eta}^{+},\\
u^{-},&\text{in }\tilde{\Omega}_{\eta}^{-},\end{cases}

is continuous across the flat interface. It satisfies, in the sense of distributions,

\operatorname{div}_{x,z}\big(\overline{\mathsf{M}}(\nabla\eta)\nabla_{x,z}u\big)=F_{\eta}\,\delta_{z=0}\qquad\text{in }\tilde{\Omega}_{\eta},

where
F_{\eta}(x)=e_{d+1}\cdot\mu_{+}^{-1}\mathsf{M}(\nabla\eta(x))\nabla_{x,z}v_{0}^{+}(x,0),

together with the boundary condition

\widetilde{\nu}^{\pm}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{M}}(\nabla\eta)\nabla_{x,z}u=0\qquad\text{on }\tilde{\Gamma}_{\eta}^{\pm}.

Here

\tilde{\Omega}_{\eta}=\left\{(x,z):\underline{b}^{-}(x)-\eta(t,x)<z<\underline{b}^{+}(x)-\eta(t,x)\right\},

and

\overline{\mathsf{M}}(\nabla\eta)=\begin{cases}\mu_{+}^{-1}\mathsf{M}(\nabla\eta),&0<z<\underline{b}^{+}(x)-\eta(t,x),\\[2.84526pt]
\mu_{-}^{-1}\mathsf{M}(\nabla\eta),&\underline{b}^{-}(x)-\eta(t,x)<z<0.\end{cases}

Since
\mathsf{M}(\nabla\eta)
is
uniformly elliptic under the Lipschitz bound on
\eta
, the standard
variational theory for uniformly elliptic divergence-form equations gives weak
solvability, up to the usual additive constant.

We next introduce the frozen transmission problem. The proof relies on a
freezing-coefficient argument, in the spirit of
[
18
]
,
adapted here to the elliptic transmission structure. The frozen problem can be
solved explicitly at the level of the boundary kernel, and its contribution
yields the leading singular operator in the evolution of
\eta
. The difference
between the true velocity and the frozen velocity will be treated as a
remainder.

To isolate the principal part, we freeze the matrix
\mathsf{M}(\nabla\eta)
at a
constant slope. More precisely, for each fixed
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let

\tilde{v}^{\pm}_{\mathbf{b}}=\tilde{v}^{\pm}_{\mathbf{b}}[\mathcal{G}]
solve

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}_{x,z}(\mathsf{M}(\mathbf{b})\nabla_{x,z}\tilde{v}^{\pm}_{\mathbf{b}})=0,\ \ \text{in}\ \mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\pm},

(3.6)

\displaystyle\tilde{v}^{-}_{\mathbf{b}}-\tilde{v}^{+}_{\mathbf{b}}=\mathcal{G}\quad\text{ on }\{z=0\},

\displaystyle e_{d+1}\cdot(\mathsf{M}(\mathbf{b})(\mu_{+}^{-1}\nabla_{x,z}\tilde{v}^{+}_{\mathbf{b}}-\mu_{-}^{-1}\nabla_{x,z}\tilde{v}^{-}_{\mathbf{b}}))=0\quad\text{ on }\{z=0\},

where we denote
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}[\eta]=-\operatorname{div}\left(\frac{\nabla\eta}{\langle\nabla\eta\rangle}\right)
.
Taking the Fourier transform in the tangential variable
x
, and denoting

\displaystyle V^{\pm}_{\mathbf{b}}(\xi,z)=(2\pi)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\tilde{v}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\pm}(x,z)e^{-i\xi\cdot x}dx,\ \ \ \xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ \ \ \quad z\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm}.

One has

\displaystyle(1+|\mathbf{b}|^{2})\partial_{z}^{2}V^{\pm}_{\mathbf{b}}(\xi,z)-|\xi|^{2}V^{\pm}_{\mathbf{b}}(\xi,z)-2i\mathbf{b}\cdot\xi\partial_{z}V_{\mathbf{b}}^{\pm}(\xi,z)=0,\ \ \ (\xi,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\pm},

\displaystyle V^{-}_{\mathbf{b}}(\xi,0)-V^{+}_{\mathbf{b}}(\xi,0)=\hat{\mathcal{G}}(\xi),

\displaystyle\left(-\frac{V^{+}_{\mathbf{b}}}{\mu_{+}}+\frac{V^{-}_{\mathbf{b}}}{\mu_{-}}\right)i\xi\cdot\mathbf{b}+\left(\frac{\partial_{z}V^{+}_{\mathbf{b}}}{\mu_{+}}-\frac{\partial_{z}V^{-}_{\mathbf{b}}}{\mu_{-}}\right)(|\mathbf{b}|^{2}+1)=0,\ \ \ \text{on}\ \{z=0\}.

By a direct calculation, we obtain the eigenvalues of the above ODE:

\displaystyle\lambda^{\pm}=\lambda^{\pm}(\xi,\mathbf{b})=\frac{i\mathbf{b}\cdot\xi\pm\sqrt{\langle\mathbf{b}\rangle^{2}|\xi|^{2}-(\mathbf{b}\cdot\xi)^{2}}}{\langle\mathbf{b}\rangle^{2}}.

(3.7)

Imposing decay as
z\to\pm\infty
, we obtain

\displaystyle V^{+}_{\mathbf{b}}(\xi,z)=c^{+}\hat{\mathcal{G}}(\xi)e^{\lambda^{-}z},\ \ \ \ \ V^{-}_{\mathbf{b}}(\xi,z)=c^{-}\hat{\mathcal{G}}(\xi)e^{\lambda^{+}z},

(3.8)

where

\displaystyle c^{\pm}=\frac{\pm\mu_{\pm}}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}}.

Taking inverse Fourier transform to obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{v}^{\pm}_{\mathbf{b}}(x,z)=(2\pi)^{-\frac{d}{2}}c^{\pm}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hat{\mathcal{G}}(\xi)e^{\lambda^{\mp}(\xi,\mathbf{b})z}e^{ix\cdot\xi}d\xi,\quad\quad\quad z\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm}.

(3.9)

More precisely, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{v}^{\pm}_{\mathbf{b}}(x,z)=c^{\pm}\mathcal{V}_{\mathbf{b}}[\mathcal{G}](x,z),\quad\quad z\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\mathbf{b}}[\mathcal{G}](x,z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K_{\mathbf{b}}(x-y,z)\mathcal{G}(y)dy,

(3.10)

with

\displaystyle K_{\mathbf{b}}(x,z)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\left(ix\cdot\xi+\lambda^{-}(\xi,\mathbf{b})z\right)d\xi,&z\geq 0,\\
\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\left(ix\cdot\xi+\lambda^{+}(\xi,\mathbf{b})z\right)d\xi,&z<0.\end{cases}

(3.11)

See Lemma
4.4
for the explicit formula of
K_{\mathbf{b}}(x,z)
.

Moreover, it is easy to check that

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\nabla_{x}\tilde{v}_{\mathbf{b}}^{+}\right)(x,0)}{\mu_{+}}=\frac{\nabla\mathcal{G}(x)}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}}.

(3.12)

To obtain
\partial_{z}\tilde{v}^{-}_{\mathbf{b}}|_{(x,0)}
, we proceed by analyzing its Fourier transform:

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\tilde{v}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\pm}[f]=c^{\pm}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\lambda^{\mp}(\xi,\mathbf{b})e^{\lambda^{\mp}(\xi,\mathbf{b})z}\hat{\mathcal{G}}[f](\xi)\right).

(3.13)

We first record two elementary facts which will be used to identify the normal
derivative of the frozen transmission solution at the flat interface. The first
lemma computes the anisotropic constant appearing in the singular-integral
representation of the Fourier multiplier

\frac{\sqrt{\langle\mathbf{b}\rangle^{2}|\xi|^{2}-(\mathbf{b}\cdot\xi)^{2}}}{\langle\mathbf{b}\rangle^{2}}.

The second lemma then converts this multiplier into a principal value operator
in physical space.

Lemma 3.2

Let
e\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. There holds,

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1-\cos(e\cdot\alpha)}{\left((\alpha\cdot\mathbf{b})^{2}+|\alpha|^{2}\right)^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}d\alpha=\frac{(\langle\mathbf{b}\rangle^{2}-(\mathbf{b}\cdot e)^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\langle\mathbf{b}\rangle^{2}},

(3.14)

where
\tilde{c}_{d}=\pi^{-\frac{d+1}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d+1}{2}\right)>0
.

Proof.
By the normalization

\tilde{c}_{d}=\pi^{-\frac{d+1}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d+1}{2}\right),

we have

\tilde{c}_{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1-\cos(\alpha_{1})}{|\alpha|^{d+1}}\,d\alpha=1.

For
d=1
, since
e=\pm 1
, the identity reduces to

\tilde{c}_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1-\cos\alpha}{(1+|\mathbf{b}|^{2})|\alpha|^{2}}\,d\alpha=\frac{1}{1+|\mathbf{b}|^{2}},

which proves the claim.

Assume now
d\geq 2
. If
\mathbf{b}
is parallel to
e
, the result follows directly by a
one-dimensional scaling in the
e
direction, or by continuity from the
non-parallel case. Hence we may assume that
\mathbf{b}
is not parallel to
e
.

We first use the following elementary rotation identity: for any

\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and any integrable function
G
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(\theta_{1}\cdot x,\theta_{2}\cdot x,|x|)dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G((1-(\theta_{1}\cdot\theta_{2})^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}x_{1}+(\theta_{1}\cdot\theta_{2})x_{2},x_{2},|x|)dx.

(3.15)

Indeed, the unit vectors

\theta_{2},\tilde{\theta}_{1}=\frac{\theta_{1}-(\theta_{1}\cdot\theta_{2})\theta_{2}}{(1-(\theta_{1}\cdot\theta_{2})^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1},

are orthogonal.
