\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\pm}(x,z)=a^{\pm}(x)J^{\pm}(x,z)\mathsf{M}(\nabla g(x))(J^{\pm}(x,z))^{\top},\ \ \ \quad\quad J^{\pm}(x,z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{Id}&0\\
-\frac{z}{a^{\pm}(x)}\nabla a^{\pm}(x)^{\top}&\frac{1}{a^{\pm}(x)}\end{array}\right).

Note that

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\pm}(x,z)-\mathsf{M}(\nabla f(x))\|_{L^{\infty}(\tilde{\Omega}_{f}^{\pm})}\lesssim\|f-g\|_{W^{1,\infty}}(1+\|g\|_{W^{1,\infty}})^{2}\lesssim\|f-g\|_{X_{T}}(1+\|g\|_{X_{T}})^{2}.

(4.48)

Denote
\mathbf{v}^{\pm}=v^{\pm}_{f}-\bar{v}_{g}^{\pm}
. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}_{x,z}({\mathsf{M}}(\nabla f)\nabla_{x,z}\mathbf{v}^{\pm})=\operatorname{div}_{x,z}(\mathbf{F}^{\pm}),\ \ \text{in}\ \tilde{\Omega}_{f}^{\pm},

(4.49)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{-}-\mathbf{v}^{+}=\mathcal{G}[f]-\mathcal{G}[g]+\varrho_{0}(f-g),\quad\text{ on }\{z=0\},

\displaystyle e_{d+1}\cdot({\mathsf{M}}(\nabla f)(\mu_{+}^{-1}\nabla_{x,z}\mathbf{v}^{+}-\mu_{-}^{-1}\nabla_{x,z}\mathbf{v}^{-}))=e_{d+1}\cdot(\mu_{+}^{-1}\mathbf{F}^{+}-\mu_{-}^{-1}\mathbf{F}^{-}),\quad\text{ on }\{z=0\},

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{f}^{\pm}\cdot({\mathsf{M}}(\nabla f)\nabla_{x,z}\mathbf{v}^{\pm})=\tilde{\nu}_{f}^{\pm}\cdot\mathbf{F}^{\pm},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{on}\ \tilde{\Gamma}^{\pm}_{f},

where
\mathbf{F}^{\pm}=(\tilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\pm}(x,z)-\mathsf{M}(\nabla f))\nabla_{x,z}\bar{v}_{g}^{\pm}
.
We have the following estimates of
\mathbf{v}^{\pm}
.

Lemma 4.15

For any
T\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, and
f,g
satisfy (
4.13
), it holds

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(t^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{+,-}\|\nabla_{x,z}\mathbf{v}^{\pm}\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega}_{f}^{\pm})}+t^{\frac{2}{3}}\sum_{+,-}\|\nabla_{x,z}\mathbf{v}^{\pm}|_{z=0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)\lesssim\|f-g\|_{X_{T}}(1+\|(f,g)\|_{X_{T}})^{10}.

Proof.
Repeating the energy estimate used in Lemma
4.10
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{+,-}\|\nabla_{x,z}\mathbf{v}^{\pm}\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega}_{f}^{\pm})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{+,-}\|(\tilde{\mathsf{M}}(x,z)-\mathsf{M}(\nabla f))\nabla_{x,z}\bar{v}_{g}^{\pm}\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega}_{f}^{\pm})}+\|\mathcal{G}[f]-\mathcal{G}[g]+\varrho_{0}(f-g)\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f-g\|_{X_{T}}(1+\|(f,g)\|_{X_{T}})^{10},

where the last inequality follows from (
4.31
), (
4.48
) and interpolation.

To control the
L^{\infty}
norm of the trace
\nabla_{x,z}\mathbf{v}^{\pm}|_{z=0}
, we apply Lemma
6.1
, which yields

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{x,z}\mathbf{v}^{\pm}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathcal{G}[f]-\mathcal{G}[g]\|_{\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}+\|f-g\|_{\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}+\|\nabla_{x,z}\mathbf{v}^{\pm}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{4r})}

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{-\frac{2}{3}}\|f-g\|_{X_{T}}(1+\|(f,g)\|_{X_{T}})^{10},

where
0<r<\frac{\mathbf{r}}{10}
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.

We have the following contraction estimate for the operator
\mathcal{Q}[f]
, which is defined in (
3.31
).

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}t^{\frac{2}{3}}\|(\mathcal{Q}[f]-\mathcal{Q}[g])(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|f-g\|_{X_{T}}(1+\|(f,g)\|_{X_{T}})^{10},

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{3}}(|\log t|^{\varkappa}\|\nabla^{m}(\mathcal{Q}[f]-\mathcal{Q}[g])(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\kappa-2}}+\|\nabla^{m}(\mathcal{Q}[f]-\mathcal{Q}[g])(t)\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}^{\kappa-2}})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\lesssim\|f-g\|_{X_{T}}(\|(f-\phi,g-\phi)\|_{X_{T}}+T^{\frac{1}{20}}\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})(1+\|(f,g)\|_{X_{T}})^{2m+10}.

The
L^{\infty}
estimate follows from Lemma
4.15
and Lemma
4.9
, and the Hölder estimate can be obtained similarly as (
4.34
). This implies the following estimate of

\mathsf{H}^{im}[f]-\mathsf{H}^{im}[g]=-\frac{1}{\mu_{+}}e_{d+1}\cdot\left((\mathsf{M}(\nabla f)-\mathsf{M}(\nabla g))\mathcal{Q}[f]+\mathsf{M}(\nabla g)(\mathcal{Q}[f]-\mathcal{Q}[g])\right).

Lemma 4.16

Let
\mathsf{H}^{im}[f]
be as defined in (
3.30
), the norm
\|\cdot\|_{X_{T}}
be as defined in (
4.2
) with constants
\varkappa,m,\kappa
fixed in (
4.1
). Then for any
T\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, and any
f,g
satisfy (
4.13
), the following estimates hold.

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\log t|^{\varkappa}(t^{\frac{\kappa}{3}}\|(\mathsf{H}^{im}[f]-\mathsf{H}^{im}[g])(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\kappa-2}}+t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{3}}\|\nabla_{x}^{m}(\mathsf{H}^{im}[f]-\mathsf{H}^{im}[g])(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\kappa-2}})

\displaystyle+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}(t^{\frac{\kappa}{3}}\|(\mathsf{H}^{im}[f]-\mathsf{H}^{im}[g])(t)\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}^{\kappa-2}}+t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{3}}\|\nabla_{x}^{m}(\mathsf{H}^{im}[f]-\mathsf{H}^{im}[g])(t)\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}^{\kappa-2}})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\lesssim\|f-g\|_{X_{T}}(\|(f-\phi,g-\phi)\|_{X_{T}}+T^{\frac{1}{20}}\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})(1+\|(f,g)\|_{X_{T}})^{2(m+10)}.

where
\mathfrak{M}_{\phi}=\|\phi\|_{H^{m+4}\cap C^{m+4}}
.

5
Proof of Theorem
1.1

We first prove a slightly more precise statement, in which the initial interface
is assumed to be close to a smooth reference profile.

Theorem 5.1

Let
\kappa
be such that
0<3-\kappa\ll 1
, and
m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
.
Take
\eta_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\varkappa>1
such that

\displaystyle\operatorname{dist}(\eta_{0},\Gamma^{\pm})>2\mathbf{r}>0.

There exists
0<\varepsilon_{0}\ll 1
such that if
\eta_{0}
further satisfies

\displaystyle\|\eta_{0}-\eta_{0}\ast\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}}\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}\leq\varepsilon_{0},

(5.1)

for some
\varepsilon_{1}>0
, then there exist
T>0
, depending on
\|\eta_{0}\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}},\|\eta_{0}-\eta_{0}\ast\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}}\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}},\mathbf{r},\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{1}
,
m
, and a unique solution
\eta
to the system (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) in
[0,T]
satisfying

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}(\|\eta(t)-\eta_{0}\ast\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}}\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}+t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{3}}|\log t|^{\varkappa}\|\nabla(\eta(t)-\eta_{0}\ast\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}})\|_{\dot{C}^{m+\kappa}})\leq C\|\eta_{0}-\eta_{0}\ast\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}}\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}},

(5.2)

\displaystyle\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\operatorname{dist}(\eta(t),\Gamma^{\pm})>\mathbf{r}.

(5.3)

We begin by deriving Theorem
1.1
from Theorem
5.1
.
Since

\eta_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{2\varkappa-1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

and
2\varkappa-1>\varkappa
, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\|\eta_{0}-\eta_{0}*\rho_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}=0.

(5.4)

Hence, choosing
\varepsilon_{1}>0
sufficiently small, condition (
5.1
)
is satisfied. Applying Theorem
5.1
, we obtain a unique solution
\eta

on some interval
[0,T]
satisfying (
5.2
) and (
1.5
).

It remains only to recover the estimate (
1.4
) in Theorem
1.1
. Since

\|\eta_{0}*\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}}\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}\lesssim\|\eta_{0}\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}},

and, for every
0<T^{\prime}\leq T
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T^{\prime}]}t^{\frac{m+\kappa}{3}}|\log t|^{\varkappa}\|\nabla(\eta_{0}*\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}})\|_{\dot{C}^{m+\kappa}}

\displaystyle\lesssim T^{\prime\frac{m+\kappa}{3}}|\log T^{\prime}|^{\varkappa}\varepsilon_{1}^{-m-1}\|\eta_{0}\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\eta_{0}\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}},

after decreasing
T^{\prime}
if necessary. This implies (
1.4
) and
Theorem
1.1
follows.

We now prove Theorem
5.1
. The proof consists of three steps: a priori
estimates, construction by smooth approximation, and stability.

Step 1: A priori Estimates

We first take

\displaystyle\phi=\eta_{0}\ast\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}},

(5.5)

and consider the system (
3.29
).
By the condition (
5.1
), we have
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and

\displaystyle\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}\leq\varepsilon_{0}.

(5.6)

We first establish a priori estimates for the solution to (
3.29
). Let
T\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, and
\eta\in C([0,\bar{T}];L^{2})\cap L^{\infty}((0,\bar{T}];W^{m,\infty})
be a solution to (
3.29
) with initial data
\eta_{0}
. Suppose (
4.13
) holds.

Let
0<\varepsilon_{0}\ll 1
be a parameter that will be fixed later in the proof. And denote
\phi=\eta_{0}\ast\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}}
such that

\displaystyle\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{in}:=\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}\leq\varepsilon_{0}.

By adding some terms on both sides of (
3.29
), and taking one spatial derivative
\partial_{i}=\partial_{x_{i}}
,
i=1,2,\cdots,d
, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\partial_{i}(\eta-\phi)(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{A}(\nabla\phi(x),\alpha):{\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{2}\partial_{i}}(\eta-\phi)(x)\frac{d\alpha}{|\alpha|^{d+1}}=\partial_{i}\mathsf{H}[\eta](x)+R_{i}[\eta](x),

(5.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{i}(\eta-\phi)|_{t=0}=\partial_{i}(\eta_{0}-\phi),

where

\displaystyle R_{i}[\eta](x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{x_{i}}\left(\mathsf{A}(\nabla\phi(x),\alpha)\right):\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{2}(\eta-\phi)(x)\frac{d\alpha}{|\alpha|^{d+1}}+\partial_{i}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{A}(\nabla\phi(x),\alpha):{\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{2}}\phi(x)\frac{d\alpha}{|\alpha|^{d+1}}\right).

(5.8)

The term
R[g]
appears when we differentiate the frozen-coefficient equation
for
g-\phi
. Since the coefficients are frozen at the smooth profile
\phi
,
this term contains only derivatives of
\phi
and is therefore lower order. The
following lemma shows that its contribution is small on short time intervals.

Lemma 5.2

Let
\|\cdot\|_{T}
be as defined in (
4.2
) with constants
\varkappa,m,\kappa
fixed in (
4.1
). Then for any
T\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, there holds

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|R[g](\tau)\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}\cap L^{2}}d\tau+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}t^{\frac{m}{3}+1}\left(|\log t|^{\varkappa}\|\nabla^{m}R[g](t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla^{m}R[g](t)\|_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\quad\quad\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{6}}(1+\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})^{m+3}(\|g-\phi\|_{T}+1),

(5.9)

where
\mathfrak{M}_{\phi}=\|\phi\|_{H^{m+4}\cap C^{m+4}}
.

Proof.
By symmetry, we have

\displaystyle R[g](x)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla_{x}\left(\mathsf{A}(\nabla\phi(x),\alpha)\right):\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\nabla^{2}(g-\phi)(x)\frac{d\alpha}{|\alpha|^{d}}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{A}(\nabla\phi(x),\alpha):\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\nabla^{2}\phi(x)\frac{d\alpha}{|\alpha|^{d}}\right).

Then by Lemma
4.2
, it is easy to check that

\displaystyle\|R[g](t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})^{3}t^{-\frac{2}{3}}(\|g-\phi\|_{T}+1),

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{m}R[g](t)\|_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})^{m+3}|\log t|^{-\varkappa}t^{-\frac{m+2}{3}}(\|g-\phi\|_{T}+1).

Hence, we obtain (
5.9
).
The proof is complete.

We next estimate the lower-order
L^{2}
component in the
X_{T}
norm.

Lemma 5.3

Let
T\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, and
f
be a solution to the Cauchy problem (
3.29
) satisfying (
4.13
). Then

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t)-\phi\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{L^{2}}+CT^{\frac{1}{10}}\|f\|_{X_{T}}(1+\|f\|_{X_{T}})^{2}.

(5.10)

Proof.
We have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(f-\phi)(x)=-\frac{1}{\mu_{+}}e_{d+1}\cdot(\mathsf{M}(\nabla f)\nabla_{x,z}v^{+}_{f}(x,z)|_{z=0}).

Using
f-\phi
as a test function to the above equation, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\big(\|f(t)-\phi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\mu_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e_{d+1}\cdot(\mathsf{M}(\nabla f)\nabla_{x,z}v^{+}_{f}(x,z)|_{z=0})(f-\phi)(x)dx.

By the trace extension theorem, there exists
\bar{f}:\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\|\bar{f}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+})}\lesssim\|f-\phi\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\ \ \ \ \ \ \bar{f}(x,0)=f(x)-\phi(x).

Note that
\operatorname{div}_{x,z}\left(\mathsf{M}(\nabla f)\nabla_{x,z}v^{+}_{f}(x,z)\right)=0
. Hence, by Hölder’s inequality and (
4.31
),

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e_{d+1}\cdot(\mathsf{M}(\nabla f)\nabla_{x,z}v^{+}_{f}(x,z)|_{z=0})(f-\phi)(x)dx\right|

(5.11)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu_{+}}\left|\int_{\tilde{\Omega}_{f}^{+}}\operatorname{div}_{x,z}\left(\mathsf{M}(\nabla f)\nabla_{x,z}v^{+}_{f}(x,z)\bar{f}(x,z)\right)dxdz\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\tilde{\Omega}_{f}^{+}}\left|(\nabla_{x,z}\bar{f})^{\top}\mathsf{M}(\nabla f)\nabla_{x,z}v^{+}_{f}(x,z)\right|dxdz\lesssim(1+\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}})^{2}\|\nabla_{x,z}\bar{f}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+})}\|\nabla_{x,z}v_{f}^{+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+})}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}})^{2}\|f-\phi\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\nabla_{x,z}v_{f}^{+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+})}\lesssim t^{-\frac{2}{3}}(1+\|f\|_{X_{T}})^{2}\|f-\phi\|_{X_{T}}\|f\|_{X_{T}}.

Integrating in time yields (
5.10
).
This completes the proof of the lemma.

We now combine the estimates above to obtain the a priori bound for

\|\eta-\phi\|_{X_{T}}
. The Schauder estimate controls the logarithmic Hölder part
of the norm, while Lemma
5.3
gives the lower-order
L^{2}
bound. Applying Lemma
2.2
, we obtain that there exists
T=T(\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\eta-\phi\|_{T}\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}\cap H^{1}}+\mathsf{Data}_{T}(\mathsf{H}[\eta],R[\eta])+\mathsf{Data}_{T}^{2}(\mathsf{H}[\eta],R[\eta]),

(5.12)

where
\mathsf{Data}_{T},\mathsf{Data}_{T}^{p}
are defined in (
2.4
) and (
2.5
).
By Lemma
4.3
, Lemma
4.13
and Lemma
5.2
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Data}_{T}(\mathsf{H}[\eta],R[\eta])\lesssim

\displaystyle(\|\eta-\phi\|_{X_{T}}+T^{\frac{1}{20}}\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})^{2}(1+\|(\eta,\phi)\|_{T})^{2(m+10)}

(5.13)

\displaystyle+T^{\frac{1}{6}}(1+\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})^{m+3}(\|\eta-\phi\|_{X_{T}}+1).

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\eta-\phi\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}\cap H^{1}}+C_{1}(\|\eta-\phi\|_{X_{T}}+T^{\frac{1}{20}}\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})^{2}(1+\|(\eta,\phi)\|_{T})^{2(m+10)}

(5.14)

\displaystyle+C_{1}T^{\frac{1}{6}}(1+\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})^{m+3}(\|\eta-\phi\|_{X_{T}}+1).

On the other hand, the
L^{2}
norm is bounded by Lemma
5.3
:

\displaystyle\|\eta-\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}}\leq\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{L^{2}}+C_{1}T^{\frac{1}{10}}(1+\|\eta\|_{X_{T}})^{2}.

(5.15)

Then we obtain from (
5.14
) and (
5.15
) that

\displaystyle\|\eta-\phi\|_{X_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}\cap H^{1}}+C_{1}(\|\eta-\phi\|_{X_{T}}+T^{\frac{1}{20}}\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})^{2}(1+\|(\eta,\phi)\|_{T})^{2(m+10)}

(5.16)

\displaystyle+C_{1}T^{\frac{1}{6}}(1+\mathfrak{M}_{\phi}+\|\eta-\phi\|_{X_{T}})^{m+5}.

We also record the propagation of the separation condition. Since the interface
moves only by the amount
|\eta(t)-\eta_{0}|
, and we have

\displaystyle\|\eta(t)-\eta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{\infty}}\leq\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\eta-\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{\infty}}\leq\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}+\|\eta-\phi\|_{X_{T}}.

Then we obtain

\displaystyle\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\operatorname{dist}(\eta(t),\Gamma^{\pm})

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{dist}(\eta_{0},\Gamma^{\pm})-\|\eta-\eta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{\infty}}

(5.17)

\displaystyle\geq 2\mathbf{r}-\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}-\|\eta-\phi\|_{X_{T}}.

Step 2: Existence

We construct solutions by smooth approximation and use the a priori estimates
from Step 1 to obtain a lifespan independent of the smoothing parameter.
For
0<\vartheta\ll 1
, denote

\displaystyle\eta_{0\vartheta}=\eta_{0}\ast\rho_{\vartheta}.

Applying the well-posedness theory in
[
48
]
, there exists
T_{\vartheta}>0
and a unique solution
\eta_{\vartheta}\in C([0,T_{\vartheta}];H^{d+1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
to the system (
1.1
)-(
1.2
). Without loss of generality, let

\displaystyle T_{\vartheta}=\sup\{T>0:\|\eta_{\vartheta}-\phi\|_{X_{T}}\leq 10C_{1}\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{in},\ \inf_{t\in[0,T]}\operatorname{dist}(\eta_{\vartheta}(t),\Gamma^{\pm})\geq\mathbf{r}\}.

(5.18)

Note that we can take
\vartheta
small enough such that
\|\eta_{0\vartheta}-\phi\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}\leq 2\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{in}\leq 2\varepsilon_{0}
.
We claim that
T_{\vartheta}
has a positive lower bound that is independent of
\vartheta
:

\displaystyle T_{\vartheta}\geq T^{\prime}:=\left(\frac{\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{in}}{100+C_{1}+\mathfrak{M}_{\phi}}\right)^{100m}.

(5.19)

We prove (
5.19
) by contradiction. If
T_{\vartheta}<T^{\prime}
, by (
5.16
) and (
5.17
),
we know that

\displaystyle\|\eta_{\vartheta}-\phi\|_{X_{T_{\vartheta}}}\leq

\displaystyle C_{1}\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{in}+C_{1}(\|\eta_{\vartheta}-\phi\|_{X_{T_{\vartheta}}}+{T_{\vartheta}}^{\frac{1}{20}}\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})^{2}(1+\|(\eta_{\vartheta},\phi)\|_{T_{\vartheta}})^{2(m+10)}

\displaystyle\quad+C_{1}{T_{\vartheta}}^{\frac{1}{6}}(1+\mathfrak{M}_{\phi}+\|\eta_{\vartheta}-\phi\|_{X_{T_{\vartheta}}})^{m+5}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2C_{1}\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{in}+C_{1}(10C_{1}\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{in}+{T_{\vartheta}}^{\frac{1}{20}}\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})^{2}(1+10C_{1}\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{in}+{T_{\vartheta}}^{\frac{1}{20}}\mathfrak{M}_{\phi}),

and

\displaystyle\inf_{t\in[0,T_{\vartheta}]}\operatorname{dist}(\eta_{\vartheta}(t),\Gamma^{\pm})

\displaystyle\geq 2\mathbf{r}-\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{in}-\|\eta_{\vartheta}-\phi\|_{X_{T_{\vartheta}}}.

By taking
\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{in}\leq\varepsilon_{0}:=(100+C_{1}+\mathbf{r})^{-100}
, we obtain

\displaystyle C_{1}(10C_{1}\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{in}+{T_{\vartheta}}^{\frac{1}{20}}\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})^{2}(1+10C_{1}\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{in}+{T_{\vartheta}}^{\frac{1}{20}}\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})\leq 3\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{in},

\displaystyle\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{in}+\|\eta-\phi\|_{X_{T_{\vartheta}}}\leq\frac{\mathbf{r}}{2}.

This implies

\displaystyle\|\eta_{\vartheta}-\phi\|_{X_{T_{\vartheta}}}\leq 5C_{1}\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{in},\quad\quad\quad\inf_{t\in[0,T_{\vartheta}]}\operatorname{dist}(\eta(t),\Gamma^{\pm})\geq\frac{3\mathbf{r}}{2}.

This contradicts the definition of
T_{\vartheta}
in (
5.18
). Hence, we obtain (
5.19
).

On the other hand, by the equation (
3.29
), we derive that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T_{\vartheta}]}t^{\frac{2}{3}}\|\partial_{t}\eta_{\vartheta}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|\eta_{\vartheta}\|_{X_{T_{\vartheta}}}(1+\|\eta_{\vartheta}\|_{X_{T_{\vartheta}}})^{10}<\infty.

Hence by Arzelà-Ascoli lemma,
\{\eta_{\vartheta}\}
has a subsequence that converges to a function
\eta
on
[0,T^{\prime}]
, which is a solution to (
3.29
) with initial data
\eta_{0}
, and satisfies the estimate

\displaystyle\|\eta-\phi\|_{X_{T}}\leq 10C_{1}\|\eta_{0}-\phi\|_{in},\ \quad\quad\operatorname{dist}(\eta(t),\Gamma^{\pm})\geq\mathbf{r}.

(5.20)

Step 3: Stability

We finally prove stability with respect to the initial data, which in particular
implies uniqueness.
Let
f,g
be the solutions to the Cauchy problem (
3.29
) associated with initial data
f_{0},g_{0}
, respectively. Then

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(f-g)(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{A}(\nabla\phi(x),\alpha):\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{2}(f-g)(x)\frac{d\alpha}{|\alpha|^{d+1}}=(\mathsf{H}[f]-\mathsf{H}[g])(x),

\displaystyle(f-g)|_{t=0}=f_{0}-g_{0}.

Applying Lemma
2.2
to the equation of
\nabla(f-g)
yields

\displaystyle\|f-g\|_{T}\lesssim\|f_{0}-g_{0}\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}+\mathsf{Data}_{T}(\mathsf{H}[f]-\mathsf{H}[g],\tilde{R}[f,g]),

where

\displaystyle\tilde{R}[f,g]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla(\mathsf{A}(\nabla\phi(x),\alpha)):\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{2}(f-g)(x)\frac{d\alpha}{|\alpha|^{d+1}}.

From Lemma
5.2
, we know that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Data}_{T}(0,\tilde{R}[f,g])\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{6}}(1+\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})\|f-g\|_{X_{T}}.

By Lemma
4.14
and Lemma
4.16
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Data}_{T}(\mathsf{H}[f]-\mathsf{H}[g],0)\lesssim\|f-g\|_{X_{T}}(\|(f-\phi,g-\phi)\|_{X_{T}}+T^{\frac{1}{20}}\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})(1+\|(f,g)\|_{X_{T}})^{2(m+10)}.

Finally, we estimate the
L^{2}
norm of
f-g
.
Note that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(f-g)=-\frac{1}{\mu_{+}}e_{d+1}\cdot((\mathsf{M}(\nabla f)-\tilde{\mathsf{M}}(x,z))\nabla_{x,z}\bar{v}_{g}^{+}|_{z=0})-\frac{1}{\mu_{+}}e_{d+1}\cdot(\mathsf{M}(\nabla f)\nabla_{x,z}\mathbf{v}^{+}|_{z=0}),

where
\tilde{M}
,
\bar{v}_{g}^{\pm}
and
\mathbf{v}^{\pm}
are defined in (
4.3
), (
4.45
) and (
4.49
) respectively.
Similar to Lemma
5.3
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|(f-g)(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\|f_{0}-g_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|f-g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}(\|\nabla(f-g)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla_{x,z}\bar{v}^{-}_{g}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{f}^{-})}+\|\nabla_{x,z}\mathbf{v}^{-}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{f}^{-})})d\tau.

Combining this with Lemma
4.10
and Lemma
4.15
yields

\displaystyle\|(f-g)(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\|f_{0}-g_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+t^{\frac{1}{3}}\|f-g\|_{X_{T}}^{2}(1+\|(f,g)\|_{X_{T}})^{10}.

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\|f-g\|_{X_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq\|f-g\|_{T}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(f-g)(t)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\|f_{0}-g_{0}\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}+C_{2}\|f-g\|_{X_{T}}(\|(f-\phi,g-\phi)\|_{X_{T}}+T^{\frac{1}{20}}\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})(1+\|(f,g)\|_{X_{T}})^{2(m+10)}

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{2}\|f_{0}-g_{0}\|_{H^{1}\cap\dot{C}^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}},

provided

\displaystyle(\|(f-\phi,g-\phi)\|_{X_{T}}+T^{\frac{1}{20}}\mathfrak{M}_{\phi})(1+\|(f,g)\|_{X_{T}})^{2(m+10)}\leq(100C_{2})^{-1}.

This completes the proof of Theorem
5.1
.

6
Appendix

In this appendix we prove the endpoint elliptic estimates used in the paper. We consider second-order divergence-form elliptic equations with coefficients in the log-Dini class
C^{\log^{\varkappa}}
,
\varkappa>1
. The proof is based on a freezing-coefficient argument together with elementary estimates for the frozen half-space kernels. Related endpoint estimates for elliptic equations can be found in
[
31
]
.

We start from the Laplace’s equation, for which the fundamental solution satisfies

\displaystyle-\Delta\Phi(x)=\delta(x)~~\text{in}~~\mathbb{R}^{d}.

where
\delta_{0}
is the Dirac mass at the origin.

The fundamental solution has an explicit formula

\Phi(x):=\begin{cases}&-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x|,\ \ \ d=2\\
&\frac{1}{d(d-2)V(d)}\frac{1}{|x|^{d-2}},\ \ \ d\geq 3.\end{cases}\ \ \ \forall\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\}.

(6.1)

Here
V(d)
is the volume of the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

We will sometimes slightly abuse notation and write
\Phi(x)=\Phi(|x|)
to emphasize that the fundamental solution is radial. We have the estimates

\displaystyle|\nabla^{k}\Phi(x)|\lesssim\frac{1}{|x|^{d-2+k}},\ \ \ \forall k\in\mathbb{N}^{+},

(6.2)

\displaystyle|\delta_{\alpha}\nabla^{k}\Phi(x)|\lesssim\left(\frac{1}{|x|^{d-2+k}}+\frac{1}{|x-\alpha|^{d-2+k}}\right)\min\left\{1,\frac{|\alpha|}{|x|}\right\},\quad\quad\quad\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(6.3)

Moreover, the fundamental solution has the following cancellation property

\displaystyle\int_{|x|=r}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\Phi(x)dS(x)=0,\ \ \ \forall\ r>0,\ \ \forall\ i,j=1,\cdots,d.

(6.4)

For the Poisson equation

\displaystyle\Delta u=\operatorname{div}f.

(6.5)

The solution reads
u(x)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Phi(x-y)\operatorname{div}f(y)dy
.
By the classical Schauder theory, for
0<\alpha<1
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\alpha(1-\alpha)}\|f\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}.

However, the
L^{\infty}\to L^{\infty}
estimate does not hold. To control the
L^{\infty}
norm of
\nabla u
, we need the
\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}
norm of
f
. In fact, by (
6.4
) and integrating by parts, we can obtain

\displaystyle\nabla u(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla^{2}\Phi(x-y)\cdot f(y)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{|x-y|\leq 1}\nabla^{2}\Phi(x-y)\cdot(f(y)-f(x))dy+\int_{|x-y|\geq 1}\nabla^{2}\Phi(x-y)\cdot f(y)dy

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=I_{1}+I_{2}.

It is easy to check that

\displaystyle|I_{1}|\lesssim\int_{|x|\leq 1}\frac{1}{|x|^{d}\log^{\varkappa}(|x|^{-1}+10)}dx\|f\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}},

provided
\varkappa>1
. For lower frequency, we have

\displaystyle|I_{2}|\lesssim\int_{|x-y|\geq 1}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{d}}|f(y)|dy\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}}.

Hence we obtain the Lipschitz estimate to the Poisson equation (
6.5
)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}+\|f\|_{L^{2}}.

(6.6)

We remark that, when considering elliptic equation with variable coefficients, the classical method to estimate equations with non-constant coefficient is to combine the standard freezing coefficient method together with the estimate of constant coefficient equations. But this fails when we estimate the endpoint
L^{\infty}
norm. Because the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}}
on the right hand side and the
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}}
norm on the left hand side of (
6.6
) do not match. Hence the remainder term for freezing coefficient cannot be absorbed by the main term on the left hand side. To obtain the
L^{\infty}
estimate of solution to (
6.5
), we need to find a suitable way to freeze the coefficient and estimate carefully.

We next derive a representation formula for solutions to an elliptic
transmission problem with variable coefficients. For clarity, we first introduce
the notation

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\tau)\ \text{if}\ \tau>0,\ \ \mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\tau,0)\ \text{if}\ \tau<0.

Consider the system

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}_{x,z}(A(x)\nabla_{x,z}u(x,z))=\operatorname{div}_{x,z}g(x,z),\ \ \text{in}\ \mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r},

(6.7)

\displaystyle u(x,0^{-})-u(x,0^{+})=h(x),\ \ \text{on}\ \{z=0\},

\displaystyle e_{d+1}\cdot(\mu_{+}^{-1}(A(x)\nabla_{x,z}u(x,0^{+})-g(x,0^{+}))-\mu_{-}^{-1}(A(x)\nabla_{x,z}u(x,0^{-})-g(x,0^{-})))=0,\ \ \text{on}\ \{z=0\}.

Suppose the variable coefficient
A(x)\in C^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{(d+1)\times(d+1)}_{sym})
is uniformly elliptic:

c_{0}\mathrm{Id}\leq A(x)\leq c_{0}^{-1}\mathrm{Id},\ \ \forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Fix
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Choose an invertible matrix

Q_{x_{0}}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d+1)\times(d+1)}
such that

\displaystyle Q_{x_{0}}Q_{x_{0}}^{\top}=A(x_{0}),\qquad(Q_{x_{0}})_{d+1,i}=0,\quad i=1,\dots,d.

(6.8)

Such a matrix
Q_{x_{0}}
can be chosen as follows. Starting from any square root
of
A(x_{0})
, we multiply it on the left by a suitable orthogonal matrix so that
the last row is parallel to
e_{d+1}
. Set

R:=\operatorname{diag}(I_{d},-1).

For
X=(x,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
and

Y=(y,w)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
, define the reflected point

Y^{*,x_{0}}:=Q_{x_{0}}RQ_{x_{0}}^{-1}Y.

Equivalently, if

Q_{x_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}B_{x_{0}}&b_{x_{0}}\\
0&q_{x_{0}}\end{pmatrix},

then

\displaystyle Y^{*,x_{0}}=\left(y-2\frac{b_{x_{0}}}{q_{x_{0}}}w,\,-w\right).

(6.9)

We define the frozen Dirichlet and Neumann Green functions associated with
the constant matrix
A(x_{0})
by

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{x_{0}}^{D}(X,Y)

\displaystyle:=|\det Q_{x_{0}}|^{-1}\left[\Phi\left(Q_{x_{0}}^{-1}(X-Y)\right)-\Phi\left(Q_{x_{0}}^{-1}(X-Y^{*,x_{0}})\right)\right],

(6.10)

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{x_{0}}^{N}(X,Y)

\displaystyle:=|\det Q_{x_{0}}|^{-1}\left[\Phi\left(Q_{x_{0}}^{-1}(X-Y)\right)+\Phi\left(Q_{x_{0}}^{-1}(X-Y^{*,x_{0}})\right)\right].

Since
Q_{x_{0}}^{-1}Y^{*,x_{0}}=RQ_{x_{0}}^{-1}Y
, these kernels can equivalently be written as

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{x_{0}}^{D}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=|\det Q_{x_{0}}|^{-1}\left[\Phi\left(Q_{x_{0}}^{-1}X-Q_{x_{0}}^{-1}Y\right)-\Phi\left(Q_{x_{0}}^{-1}X-RQ_{x_{0}}^{-1}Y\right)\right],

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{x_{0}}^{N}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=|\det Q_{x_{0}}|^{-1}\left[\Phi\left(Q_{x_{0}}^{-1}X-Q_{x_{0}}^{-1}Y\right)+\Phi\left(Q_{x_{0}}^{-1}X-RQ_{x_{0}}^{-1}Y\right)\right].

By construction, these kernels satisfy the boundary conditions on
\{w=0\}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{x_{0}}^{D}(X,Y)\big|_{w=0}=0,

(6.11)

\displaystyle e_{d+1}\cdot A(x_{0})\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}_{x_{0}}^{N}(X,Y)\big|_{w=0}=0.

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}_{y,w}\bigl(A(x_{0})\nabla_{y,w}\mathsf{G}_{x_{0}}^{D}(x,z,y,w)\bigr)=-\delta_{(x,z)}(y,w)+\delta_{(x,z)^{*,x_{0}}}(y,w),

(6.12)

and

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}_{y,w}\bigl(A(x_{0})\nabla_{y,w}\mathsf{G}_{x_{0}}^{N}(x,z,y,w)\bigr)=-\delta_{(x,z)}(y,w)-\delta_{(x,z)^{*,x_{0}}}(y,w),

where the identities are understood in the sense of distributions.

We now state the representation formula obtained by freezing the coefficient
matrix at a fixed tangential point
x_{0}
. The formula separates three
contributions: the bulk forcing, the jump data on the interface, and an
artificial-boundary term coming from the localization in the normal variable.
The latter is lower order and is controlled by energy norms.

Lemma 6.1

Fix
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
\mathsf{G}_{x_{0}}^{D}
and
\mathsf{G}_{x_{0}}^{N}
be the frozen Dirichlet and
Neumann kernels defined in (
6.10
), and let
X^{*,x_{0}}
be the corresponding
reflected point.
Assume that
u,g,h
are smooth and decay sufficiently fast in the
tangential variables, and that
u
solves (
6.7
). Then
for every
X=(x,z)\in\mathbb{R}_{r}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-r}^{d+1}
, one has

\displaystyle u(X)=

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{+}\mathbf{1}_{\{z>0\}}+\mu_{-}\mathbf{1}_{\{z<0\}}}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}}\left[\frac{1}{r}\int_{r}^{2r}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau}}\mathcal{H}_{x_{0}}(Y)\cdot\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}^{D}_{x_{0}}(X,Y)\,dY\,d\tau\right.

(6.13)

\displaystyle\left.-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e_{d+1}\cdot A(x_{0})\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}^{D}_{x_{0}}(X,(y,0))h(y)\,dy\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{\mu_{+}\mu_{-}}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}}\sum_{\pm}\frac{1}{r\mu_{\pm}}\int_{r}^{2r}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\pm\tau}^{d+1}}\mathcal{H}_{x_{0}}(Y)\cdot\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}^{N}_{x_{0}}(X,Y)\,dY\,d\tau

\displaystyle+\mathrm{LO}(u,g)(X),

where

\mathcal{H}_{x_{0}}(Y):=g(Y)+\bigl(A(x_{0})-A(y)\bigr)\nabla_{Y}u(Y).

The term
\mathrm{LO}(u,g)
is the averaged artificial-boundary
contribution which satisfies

\left\|\nabla_{X}^{n}\mathrm{LO}(u,g)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{r/2}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-r/2}^{d+1})}\leq C(r,n,c_{0},d)\left(\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{2r}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-2r}^{d+1})}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{2r}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-2r}^{d+1})}\right),\quad\quad\forall\ n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}.

(6.14)

Proof.
We write
A_{0}:=A(x_{0})
and

\mathcal{E}_{x_{0}}(Y):=\bigl(A_{0}-A(y)\bigr)\nabla_{Y}u(Y).

Then

A(y)\nabla_{Y}u=A_{0}\nabla_{Y}u-\mathcal{E}_{x_{0}},\qquad\mathcal{H}_{x_{0}}=g+\mathcal{E}_{x_{0}}.

All integrations by parts below are first justified with a tangential
cutoff and by excising small balls around the poles of the frozen
kernels. The cutoff is then removed and the radii of the excised balls are
sent to zero. We suppress these standard limiting steps in the notation.

Fix
\tau\in(r,2r)
. We first record the artificial-boundary terms.
For

\mathcal{S}\in\{D,N\}
, define

\displaystyle\mathrm{LO}_{\tau,\pm}^{\mathcal{S}}(X):=

\displaystyle\pm\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e_{d+1}\cdot\bigl(A(y)\nabla_{Y}u-g\bigr)(y,\pm\tau)\,\mathsf{G}_{x_{0}}^{\mathcal{S}}(X,(y,\pm\tau))\,dy

(6.15)

\displaystyle\mp\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(y,\pm\tau)\,e_{d+1}\cdot A_{0}\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}_{x_{0}}^{\mathcal{S}}(X,(y,\pm\tau))\,dy.

Using the Neumann kernel in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}}(X)u(X)+\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}}(X^{*,x_{0}})u(X^{*,x_{0}})

(6.16)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}}\mathcal{H}_{x_{0}}(Y)\cdot\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}^{N}_{x_{0}}(X,Y)\,dY-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e_{d+1}\cdot(A\nabla_{Y}u-g)(y,0^{+})\mathsf{G}^{N}_{x_{0}}(X,(y,0))\,dy+\mathrm{LO}_{\tau,+}^{N}(X).

The minus sign in the interface term comes from the fact that the outward
normal of
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}
on
\{w=0\}
is
-e_{d+1}
.

Similarly, using the Neumann kernel in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau}
, whose outward
normal on
\{w=0\}
is
e_{d+1}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau}}(X)u(X)+\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau}}(X^{*,x_{0}})u(X^{*,x_{0}})

(6.17)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau}}\mathcal{H}_{x_{0}}(Y)\cdot\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}^{N}_{x_{0}}(X,Y)\,dY+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e_{d+1}\cdot(A\nabla_{Y}u-g)(y,0^{-})\mathsf{G}^{N}_{x_{0}}(X,(y,0))\,dy+\mathrm{LO}_{\tau,-}^{N}(X).

Multiplying (
6.16
) by
\mu_{+}^{-1}
, multiplying
(
6.17
) by
\mu_{-}^{-1}
, and adding the two formulas, the
interface flux terms cancel by the transmission condition in
(
6.7
). Therefore

\displaystyle\mu_{+}^{-1}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}}(X)u(X)+\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}}(X^{*,x_{0}})u(X^{*,x_{0}})\right]

(6.18)

\displaystyle+\mu_{-}^{-1}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau}}(X)u(X)+\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau}}(X^{*,x_{0}})u(X^{*,x_{0}})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pm}\mu_{\pm}^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\pm\tau}^{d+1}}\mathcal{H}_{x_{0}}(Y)\cdot\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}^{N}_{x_{0}}(X,Y)\,dY+\mathrm{LO}_{\tau}^{N}(X),

where

\mathrm{LO}_{\tau}^{N}(X):=\mu_{+}^{-1}\mathrm{LO}_{\tau,+}^{N}(X)+\mu_{-}^{-1}\mathrm{LO}_{\tau,-}^{N}(X).

Next we use the Dirichlet kernel. Since

\mathsf{G}^{D}_{x_{0}}(X,(y,0))=0,

the interface terms containing the conormal flux vanish. In

\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}
, integration by parts gives

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}}(X)u(X)-\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}}(X^{*,x_{0}})u(X^{*,x_{0}})

(6.19)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}}\mathcal{H}_{x_{0}}(Y)\cdot\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}^{D}_{x_{0}}(X,Y)\,dY+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(y,0^{+})e_{d+1}\cdot A_{0}\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}^{D}_{x_{0}}(X,(y,0))\,dy+\mathrm{LO}_{\tau,+}^{D}(X).

Similarly, in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau}}(X)u(X)-\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau}}(X^{*,x_{0}})u(X^{*,x_{0}})

(6.20)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau}}\mathcal{H}_{x_{0}}(Y)\cdot\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}^{D}_{x_{0}}(X,Y)\,dY-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(y,0^{-})e_{d+1}\cdot A_{0}\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}^{D}_{x_{0}}(X,(y,0))\,dy+\mathrm{LO}_{\tau,-}^{D}(X).

Adding (
6.19
) and (
6.20
), and using

u(y,0^{-})-u(y,0^{+})=h(y),

we deduce that

\displaystyle u(X)-u(X^{*,x_{0}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau}}\mathcal{H}_{x_{0}}(Y)\cdot\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}^{D}_{x_{0}}(X,Y)\,dY

(6.21)

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e_{d+1}\cdot A_{0}\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}^{D}_{x_{0}}(X,(y,0))h(y)\,dy+\mathrm{LO}_{\tau}^{D}(X),

where

\mathrm{LO}_{\tau}^{D}(X):=\mathrm{LO}_{\tau,+}^{D}(X)+\mathrm{LO}_{\tau,-}^{D}(X).

(6.22)

Here, for
X\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau}
, exactly one of
X

and
X^{*,x_{0}}
lies in the upper half-domain and the other lies in the
lower half-domain.

If
X\in\mathbb{R}_{r}^{d+1}
, then (
6.18
) and (
6.21
) give

\mu_{+}^{-1}u(X)+\mu_{-}^{-1}u(X^{*,x_{0}})=\mathcal{B}_{1}(X,\tau),\qquad u(X)-u(X^{*,x_{0}})=\mathcal{B}_{2}(X,\tau),

where

\mathcal{B}_{1}(X,\tau):=\sum_{\pm}\mu_{\pm}^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\pm\tau}^{d+1}}\mathcal{H}_{x_{0}}(Y)\cdot\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}^{N}_{x_{0}}(X,Y)\,dY+\mathrm{LO}_{\tau}^{N}(X)

and

\mathcal{B}_{2}(X,\tau):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau}}\mathcal{H}_{x_{0}}(Y)\cdot\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}^{D}_{x_{0}}(X,Y)\,dY-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e_{d+1}\cdot A_{0}\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}^{D}_{x_{0}}(X,(y,0))h(y)\,dy+\mathrm{LO}_{\tau}^{D}(X).

Solving the system yields

u(X)=\frac{\mu_{+}\mu_{-}}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}}\mathcal{B}_{1}(X,\tau)+\frac{\mu_{+}}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}}\mathcal{B}_{2}(X,\tau).

If
X\in\mathbb{R}_{-r}^{d+1}
, the same identities give directly

\mu_{-}^{-1}u(X)+\mu_{+}^{-1}u(X^{*,x_{0}})=\mathcal{B}_{1}(X,\tau),\qquad u(X)-u(X^{*,x_{0}})=\mathcal{B}_{2}(X,\tau),

and hence

u(X)=\frac{\mu_{+}\mu_{-}}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}}\mathcal{B}_{1}(X,\tau)+\frac{\mu_{-}}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}}\mathcal{B}_{2}(X,\tau).

Thus, for
X=(x,z)\in\mathbb{R}_{r}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-r}^{d+1}
,

u(X)=\frac{\mu_{+}\mu_{-}}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}}\mathcal{B}_{1}(X,\tau)+\frac{\mu_{+}\mathbf{1}_{\{z>0\}}+\mu_{-}\mathbf{1}_{\{z<0\}}}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}}\mathcal{B}_{2}(X,\tau).

Averaging this identity in
\tau\in[r,2r]
gives
(
6.13
), with

\displaystyle\mathrm{LO}(u,g)(X):=

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{+}\mu_{-}}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}}\frac{1}{r}\int_{r}^{2r}\mathrm{LO}_{\tau}^{N}(X)\,d\tau

(6.23)

\displaystyle+\frac{\mu_{+}\mathbf{1}_{\{z>0\}}+\mu_{-}\mathbf{1}_{\{z<0\}}}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}}\frac{1}{r}\int_{r}^{2r}\mathrm{LO}_{\tau}^{D}(X)\,d\tau.

It remains to prove (
6.14
). For weak solutions, the
averaged artificial-boundary contributions are understood as integrals
over the slabs

\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(r,2r),\qquad\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(-2r,-r).

Let
\alpha
be a multi-index with
|\alpha|=n\geq 1
. By (
6.15
), after applying

\partial_{X}^{\alpha}
, the artificial-boundary terms containing

\nabla u
or
g
have the form

\frac{1}{r}\int_{r}^{2r}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K_{\alpha}(X,y,\pm\tau)\bigl(\nabla u(y,\pm\tau),g(y,\pm\tau)\bigr)\,dy\,d\tau.

Here
K_{\alpha}
denotes a generic kernel obtained by applying
\partial_{X}^{\alpha}
to the frozen Green kernels appearing in the artificial-boundary representation.
Since
X\in\mathbb{R}_{r/2}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-r/2}^{d+1}
and

\tau\in[r,2r]
, we have
|X-(y,\pm\tau)|\geq\frac{1}{2}r
. Moreover,
the standard decay of the frozen Green kernels and their derivatives gives

\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}_{r/2}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-r/2}^{d+1}}\left(\frac{1}{r}\int_{r}^{2r}\|K_{\alpha}(X,\cdot,\pm\tau)\|_{L^{2}_{y}}^{2}\,d\tau\right)^{1/2}\leq C(\alpha,c_{0},d)r^{-\alpha-\frac{d}{2}+1}.

By Cauchy’s inequality in
(y,\tau)
, these terms are bounded by

C(r,\alpha,c_{0},d)\left(\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{2r}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-2r}^{d+1})}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{2r}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-2r}^{d+1})}\right).

It remains to estimate the artificial-boundary terms containing
u
.
They have the form

I_{\alpha}(X)=\frac{1}{r}\int_{r}^{2r}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{X}^{\alpha}(e_{d+1}\cdot A_{0}\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}_{x_{0}}^{\mathcal{S}}(X,(y,\pm\tau)))\,u(y,\pm\tau)\,dy\,d\tau,\qquad|\alpha|\geq 1,\quad\mathcal{S}\in\{D,N\}.

We claim that

\partial_{X}^{\alpha}(e_{d+1}\cdot A_{0}\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}_{x_{0}}^{\mathcal{S}}(X,(y,\pm\tau)))=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\partial_{y_{i}}L_{\alpha,i}(X,y,\pm\tau),

(6.24)

where
L_{\alpha,i}
satisfies

\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}_{r/2}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-r/2}^{d+1}}\left(\frac{1}{r}\int_{r}^{2r}\|L_{\alpha,i}(X,\cdot,\pm\tau)\|_{L^{2}_{y}}^{2}\,d\tau\right)^{1/2}\leq C(\alpha,c_{0},d)r^{-\alpha-\frac{d}{2}+1}.

(6.25)

Indeed, for the direct part of the frozen kernels, tangential

X
-derivatives are equal to minus tangential
y
-derivatives. For
the reflected part, the same statement holds after passing to the

Q_{x_{0}}
-coordinates; equivalently, in the original variables,
tangential
X
-derivatives are constant linear combinations of
tangential
y
-derivatives. Thus tangential
X
-derivatives already
give the required tangential divergence structure.

It remains to consider normal
X
-derivatives. The only terms
which are not already tangential divergences are the pure normal second
derivatives. Since
X\in\mathbb{R}_{r/2}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-r/2}^{d+1}
and

\tau\in[r,2r]
, the points
(y,\pm\tau)
remain away from both
poles
X
and
X^{*,x_{0}}
. Therefore the frozen kernels are

A_{0}
-harmonic in a neighborhood of the artificial boundaries:

\operatorname{div}_{Y}(A_{0}\nabla_{Y}G)=0.

Writing
A_{0}=(a_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq d+1}
, this gives

a_{d+1,d+1}\partial_{ww}G+2\sum_{i=1}^{d}a_{i,d+1}\partial_{y_{i}w}G+\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a_{ij}\partial_{y_{i}y_{j}}G=0.

Since
a_{d+1,d+1}\geq c_{0}>0
, every occurrence of

\partial_{ww}G
can be rewritten as a tangential divergence. Since the pole is excluded, the kernel is smooth and (
6.25
) holds. Commuting
this identity with further
Y
-derivatives yields the same conclusion
for higher-order differentiated kernels. Therefore
(
6.24
) holds for all
|\alpha|\geq 1
.

With the expression (
6.24
), we use integration by parts in
y

to obtain

I_{\alpha}(X)=-\frac{1}{r}\int_{r}^{2r}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}L_{\alpha}(X,y,\pm\tau)\cdot\nabla_{y}u(y,\pm\tau)\,dy\,d\tau.

Consequently,

|I_{\alpha}(X)|\leq C(r,\alpha,c_{0},d)\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{2r}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-2r}^{d+1})}.

Combining the estimates for the
u
-terms, the
\nabla u
-terms, and
the
g
-terms proves (
6.14
) for every

n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
.

For later use in the estimate of the boundary term generated by the jump data, we record that the same Poisson-kernel structure remains valid for any uniformly elliptic constant matrix.

Lemma 6.2

Let
A_{*}
be a constant symmetric matrix satisfying

c_{0}\mathrm{Id}\leq A_{*}\leq c_{0}^{-1}\mathrm{Id}.

Let
\mathsf{G}^{D}_{A_{*}}
denote the Dirichlet Green function in the upper or lower half-space associated with the constant-coefficient operator

\operatorname{div}_{X}(A_{*}\nabla_{X}).

For
X=(x,z),Y=(y,w)\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\pm}
, define the corresponding boundary kernel

\mathcal{P}_{A_{*}}(X,y):=e_{d+1}\cdot(A_{*}\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}^{D}_{A_{*}}(X,Y))\big|_{w=0}.

For
h\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, set

T_{A_{*}}[h](X):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{P}_{A_{*}}(X,y)h(y)dy.

Then for
\varkappa>1
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{X}T_{A_{*}}[h]\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|h\|_{C^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}.

Proof.
Note that
\mathcal{P}_{A_{*}}
is an anisotropic Poisson kernel.
Indeed,
let

Q_{*}=\begin{pmatrix}B_{*}&b_{*}\\
0&q_{*}\end{pmatrix},

be the matrix defined in (
6.8
) associated to the matrix
A_{*}
.
By the definition of the Dirichlet Green function in (
6.10
), it is easy to check that

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A_{*}}(X,y)=|\det B_{*}|^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{Id}}(Q_{*}^{-1}X,B_{*}^{-1}y).

As a result, we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla_{X}T_{A_{*}}[h](X)=Q_{*}^{-\top}(\nabla_{X}T_{\mathrm{Id}}[h\circ B_{*}])(Q_{*}^{-1}X).

On the other hand, we have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{Id}}(x,z,y)=\operatorname{sgn}(z)K_{\mathbf{b}}(x-y,z)|_{\mathbf{b}=0},

where
K_{\mathbf{b}}
is defined in (
4.16
). From Lemma
4.8
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{X}T_{\mathrm{Id}}[h]\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|\nabla_{x,z}(K_{\mathbf{b}}(\cdot,z)\ast h)(x)|_{\mathbf{b}=0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|h\|_{C^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}},

with
\varkappa>1
. Then it follows that

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{X}T_{A_{*}}[h]\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|h\circ B_{*}\|_{C^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}}\lesssim\|h\|_{C^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}},

where the implicit constant depends only on
d,\varkappa
and the ellipticity constant
c_{0}
. The proof is complete.

We have the following lemma considering the Lipschitz estimate of the solution in Lemma
6.1
.

Lemma 6.3

Let
\varkappa>1
.
Assume that

A\in C^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{(d+1)\times(d+1)}_{\rm sym}),\qquad c_{0}\mathrm{Id}\leq A(y)\leq c_{0}^{-1}\mathrm{Id}.

There exists
r=r(d,c_{0},\mu^{\pm},\varkappa,\|A\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}})\in(0,1)
such that, if
u\in\dot{W}^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{3r}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-3r}^{d+1})
is a weak solution of (
6.7
) in

\mathbb{R}_{3r}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-3r}^{d+1}
, then

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{x,z}u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{r/2}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-r/2}^{d+1})}\leq

\displaystyle C\|h\|_{C^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C\|g\|_{C^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}_{3r}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-3r}^{d+1})}

(6.26)

\displaystyle+C(r)\left(\|\nabla_{x,z}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{3r}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-3r}^{d+1})}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{3r}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-3r}^{d+1})}\right),

where

C=C(d,c_{0},\mu_{\pm},\varkappa),C(r)=C(d,c_{0},\mu_{\pm},\varkappa,r),

and

\|g\|_{C^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}_{3r}^{d+1}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-3r}^{d+1})}:=\|g\|_{C^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}_{3r}^{d+1})}+\|g\|_{C^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}_{-3r}^{d+1})}

is understood phase by phase.

Proof.
It suffices to prove the estimate for smooth solutions with sufficient
decay in the tangential variables. The estimate for weak solutions follows
by the approximation argument in Lemma
6.1
, together with the
differentiated lower-order estimate (
6.14
).

For
\tau>0
, recall that

\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\tau),\qquad\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(-\tau,0).

For
\diamond\in\{+,-\}
, we also use the shorthand notation

\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau}:=\begin{cases}\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau},&\diamond=+,\\
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau},&\diamond=-.\end{cases}

All
C^{\log^{\varkappa}}
-norms on

\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\tau}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-\tau}
are understood
phase by phase.

We first prove the estimate in the upper phase. The lower-phase estimate is
obtained by repeating the same argument with the roles of
+
and
-

interchanged. Fix

X_{*}=(x_{*},z_{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{r/2}.

In the representation formula (
6.13
), choose

x_{0}=x_{*},\qquad A_{0}=A(x_{*}),

and keep this frozen point fixed when differentiating with respect to

X=(x,z)
. We then evaluate the differentiated identity at
X=X_{*}
.

Set

\theta_{+}:=\frac{\mu_{+}}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}},\qquad\theta_{-}:=\frac{\mu_{-}}{\mu_{+}+\mu_{-}}.

We define the frozen transmission kernel by

\mathfrak{K}_{x_{*}}^{\diamond}(X,Y):=\theta_{+}\mathsf{G}_{x_{*}}^{D}(X,Y)+\theta_{-\diamond}\mathsf{G}_{x_{*}}^{N}(X,Y),\quad\quad\diamond\in\{+,-\}.

Then the body terms in (
6.13
) can be written as

\sum_{\sigma=\pm}\frac{1}{r}\int_{r}^{2r}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau}}\mathcal{H}_{x_{*}}(Y)\cdot\nabla_{Y}\mathfrak{K}_{x_{*}}^{\diamond}(X,Y)\,dY\,d\tau,

where, for
Y=(y,w)
,

\mathcal{H}_{x_{*}}(Y):=g(Y)+\bigl(A(x_{*})-A(y)\bigr)\nabla_{Y}u(Y).

The boundary jump term is

-\theta_{+}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e_{d+1}\cdot A(x_{*})\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}_{x_{*}}^{D}(X,(y,0))h(y)\,dy,

and the remaining term is denoted by
\mathrm{LO}(u,g)(X)
.

We next record the kernel structure used below. Write

\mathsf{G}_{x_{*}}^{D}=\mathcal{K}_{x_{*}}-\mathcal{K}_{x_{*}}^{\rm ref},\qquad\mathsf{G}_{x_{*}}^{N}=\mathcal{K}_{x_{*}}+\mathcal{K}_{x_{*}}^{\rm ref},

where
\mathcal{K}_{x_{*}}
is the direct frozen fundamental solution and

\mathcal{K}_{x_{*}}^{\rm ref}
is the reflected part. Hence

\mathfrak{K}_{x_{*}}^{\diamond}=(\theta_{+}+\theta_{-\diamond})\mathcal{K}_{x_{*}}+(-\theta_{+}+\theta_{-\diamond})\mathcal{K}_{x_{*}}^{\rm ref}.

Since
X_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{r/2}
, the reflected pole is absent from
the opposite phase. More precisely,

\mathfrak{K}_{x_{*}}^{-}=2\theta_{+}\,\mathcal{K}_{x_{*}}.

The remaining direct pole may still approach the interface as
z_{*}\to 0
,
so the opposite-phase contribution is treated as a near-boundary singular
term, not as a uniformly smooth error.

For each
\diamond\in\{+,-\}
and
\tau\in[r,2r]
, let

\mathcal{P}_{\diamond,\tau}
denote the finite set of relevant poles of

\nabla_{X}\nabla_{Y}\mathfrak{K}_{x_{*}}^{\diamond}(X_{*},Y)

with respect to the phase
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau}
. These are the
poles which either lie in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau}
, or lie outside

\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau}
but have distance
\lesssim r
from this
phase. All other poles are uniformly separated and will be included in the
regular part. For
P=(p,s)\in\mathcal{P}_{\diamond,\tau}
, set

a_{P}:=\operatorname{dist}\bigl(P,\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau}\bigr).

Thus
a_{P}=0
if
P\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau}
, while
a_{P}>0
if

P
is an exterior pole. For every such pole,

|p-x_{*}|\leq Ca_{P}.

(6.27)

For the direct pole this is immediate. For the reflected pole it follows
from the explicit frozen reflection formula and from the fact that its
normal distance to the relevant phase is comparable to
|z_{*}|
.

For
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\diamond,\tau}
, define

\mathcal{N}_{P}:=\left\{Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau}:\ |Y-P|\leq cr\right\}.

On
\mathcal{N}_{P}
, one has
|Y-P|\geq a_{P}
, and the frozen kernel satisfies

\left|\nabla_{X}\nabla_{Y}\mathfrak{K}_{x_{*}}^{\diamond}(X_{*},Y)\right|\leq C|Y-P|^{-(d+1)}.

(6.28)

Here the distance may be taken either in the Euclidean metric or in the
equivalent frozen metric. Away from the union of the neighborhoods

\mathcal{N}_{P}
, the differentiated kernels have
L^{2}
-norm bounded by

C(r)
, uniformly in

X_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{r/2}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-r/2}
and

\tau\in[r,2r]
.

On
\mathcal{N}_{P}
, the singular part of
\nabla_{X}\nabla_{Y}\mathfrak{K}^{\diamond}_{x_{\ast}}(X_{\ast},Y)
generated by the pole
P
will be denoted by
\mathcal{K}_{P}(Y)
. Thus, near the relevant poles,

\nabla_{X}\nabla_{Y}\mathfrak{K}^{\diamond}_{x_{\ast}}(X_{\ast},Y)=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{\diamond,\tau}}\mathcal{K}_{P}(Y)+\mathcal{R}_{\diamond,\tau}(Y),

where
\mathcal{R}_{\diamond,\tau}
contains the pole contributions separated from the phase and the regular remainder.

We record the cancellation of
\mathcal{K}_{P}
. Define the frozen distance from
P
by

\rho_{P}(Y):=\left|Q_{x_{\ast}}^{-1}(Y-P)\right|.

After the affine change
Z=Q_{x_{\ast}}^{-1}(Y-P)
, the kernel
\mathcal{K}_{P}
is a constant matrix multiple of
\nabla^{2}\Phi(Z)
. Since
\nabla^{2}\Phi
is homogeneous of degree
-(d+1)
, we may write

\mathcal{K}_{P}(Y)=\rho_{P}(Y)^{-(d+1)}\Omega_{P}\left(\frac{Q_{x_{\ast}}^{-1}(Y-P)}{\rho_{P}(Y)}\right),

where
\Omega_{P}
is smooth on
\mathbb{S}^{d}
. From the cancellation property of
\Phi
in (
6.4
), for every
0<\varepsilon<R
,

\int_{\varepsilon<\rho_{P}(Y)<R}\mathcal{K}_{P}(Y)dY=0.

(6.29)

Thus the cancellation holds on the
A_{\ast}(x_{*})
-deformed annuli centered at
P
.

We now estimate the four terms in the differentiated representation formula.

First, consider the jump term

T_{h}(X):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e_{d+1}\cdot A(x_{*})\nabla_{Y}\mathsf{G}_{x_{*}}^{D}(X,(y,0))h(y)\,dy.

By Lemma
6.2
, we have

|\nabla_{X}T_{h}(X_{*})|\lesssim\|h\|_{C^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

(6.30)

Next consider the
g
-part:

T_{g}(X):=\sum_{\diamond=\pm}\frac{1}{r}\int_{r}^{2r}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau}}g(Y)\cdot\nabla_{Y}\mathfrak{K}_{x_{*}}^{\diamond}(X,Y)\,dY\,d\tau.

We decompose the kernel into singular pieces near the poles

P\in\mathcal{P}_{\diamond,\tau}
and a regular piece. On each singular
neighborhood
\mathcal{N}_{P}
, we subtract a phasewise constant
g_{P}
.
If
P\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau}
, we take
g_{P}=g(P)
. If

P\notin\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau}
, we choose

P^{\sharp}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau}
such that

|P^{\sharp}-P|\leq 2a_{P},\qquad|Y-P^{\sharp}|\leq|P-P^{\sharp}|+|Y-P|\leq 3|Y-P|,

and set
g_{P}=g(P^{\sharp})
.

The constant part is treated by inserting and subtracting the corresponding
principal value integral over the full frozen phase. This full singular
integral vanishes by the cancellation of the frozen Calderon–Zygmund
kernel, see (
6.29
). The error produced by the localization is supported in an annular
region separated from the pole and is therefore included in the regular
part. Hence the singular contribution is reduced to the integral with

g-g_{P}
. By the phasewise logarithmic modulus of
g
,

|g(Y)-g_{P}|\leq C\|g\|_{C^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}\log^{-\varkappa}(2+|Y-P|^{-1}).

(6.31)

Combining (
6.31
) with (
6.28
), we obtain

\int_{\mathcal{N}_{P}}\left|\nabla_{X}\nabla_{Y}\mathfrak{K}_{x_{*}}^{\sigma}(X_{*},Y)\right||g(Y)-g_{P}|\,dY\lesssim\|g\|_{C^{\log^{\varkappa}}}\int_{0}^{Cr}\frac{d\ell}{\ell\log^{\varkappa}(2+\ell^{-1})}.

Since
\varkappa>1
, the last integral is finite (note that
r<1
). The regular pieces are
estimated by Cauchy’s inequality and the
L^{2}
-bounds of the differentiated
kernels. Therefore

|\nabla_{X}T_{g}(X_{*})|\leq C\|g\|_{C^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}+C(r)\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}.

(6.32)

We now estimate the coefficient-freezing error

T_{A}(X):=\sum_{\diamond=\pm}\frac{1}{r}\int_{r}^{2r}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau}}\bigl(A(x_{*})-A(y)\bigr)\nabla_{Y}u(Y)\cdot\nabla_{Y}\mathfrak{K}_{x_{*}}^{\diamond}(X,Y)\,dY\,d\tau.

For each
\diamond
and
\tau
, choose
a smooth cutoff
\chi_{\tau}
such that

\displaystyle 0\leq\chi_{\diamond,\tau}\leq 1,\qquad\operatorname{supp}\chi_{\diamond,\tau}\subset\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{\diamond,\tau}}\mathcal{N}_{P},

\displaystyle\chi_{\diamond,\tau}\equiv 1\quad\text{on}\quad\left\{Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau}:\ \operatorname{dist}(Y,\cup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{\diamond,\tau}}\mathcal{N}_{P})\leq cr/2\right\}.

Set

\chi_{0,\diamond,\tau}:=1-\chi_{\diamond,\tau}.

We decompose

T_{A}=T_{A}^{\rm sing}+T_{A}^{\rm reg},

where

\displaystyle T_{A}^{\rm sing}(X):=\sum_{\diamond=\pm}\frac{1}{r}\int_{r}^{2r}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau}}

\displaystyle\chi_{\diamond,\tau}(Y)\bigl(A(x_{*})-A(y)\bigr)\nabla_{Y}u(Y)

\displaystyle\cdot\nabla_{Y}\mathfrak{K}_{x_{*}}^{\diamond}(X,Y)\,dY\,d\tau,

and

\displaystyle T_{A}^{\rm reg}(X):=\sum_{\diamond=\pm}\frac{1}{r}\int_{r}^{2r}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau}}

\displaystyle\chi_{0,\diamond,\tau}(Y)\bigl(A(x_{*})-A(y)\bigr)\nabla_{Y}u(Y)

\displaystyle\cdot\nabla_{Y}\mathfrak{K}_{x_{*}}^{\diamond}(X,Y)\,dY\,d\tau.

We first estimate the singular part. Let

P=(p,s)\in\mathcal{P}_{\diamond,\tau}
. For

Y=(y,w)\in\mathcal{N}_{P}
, using (
6.27
) and

|Y-P|\geq a_{P}
, we get

|y-x_{*}|\leq|y-p|+|p-x_{*}|\leq C(|Y-P|+a_{P})\leq C|Y-P|.

Therefore, by the logarithmic modulus of
A
,

|A(x_{*})-A(y)|\leq C\|A\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\log^{-\varkappa}(2+|Y-P|^{-1}),

(6.33)

where

\|A\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\sup_{x\neq y}\frac{|A(x)-A(y)|}{\log^{-\varkappa}(2+|x-y|^{-1})}.

Combining (
6.33
) with (
6.28
), we obtain

\displaystyle|\nabla_{X}T_{A}^{\rm sing}(X_{*})|

\displaystyle\leq C\|A\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\frac{1}{r}\int_{r}^{2r}\sum_{\diamond=\pm}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{\diamond,\tau}}\int_{0}^{Cr}\frac{d\ell}{\ell\log^{\varkappa}(2+\ell^{-1})}\,d\tau

\displaystyle\leq c(r)\|A\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})},

where

c(r):=C\int_{0}^{Cr}\frac{d\ell}{\ell\log^{\varkappa}(2+\ell^{-1})}\longrightarrow 0\qquad\text{as }r\to 0.

This is the only place where the log-Dini condition
\varkappa>1
is used
in an essential way.

We next estimate the regular part. On the support of

\chi_{0,\diamond,\tau}
, the kernel is separated from all relevant interior
and exterior poles. Hence

\sup_{X_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{r/2}}\left(\frac{1}{r}\int_{r}^{2r}\left\|\chi_{0,\diamond,\tau}\nabla_{X}\nabla_{Y}\mathfrak{K}_{x_{*}}^{\diamond}(X_{*},\cdot)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\diamond\tau})}^{2}\,d\tau\right)^{1/2}\leq C(r).

Since
A
is bounded, Cauchy’s inequality gives

|\nabla_{X}T_{A}^{\rm reg}(X_{*})|\leq C(r)\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}.

Consequently,

|\nabla_{X}T_{A}(X_{*})|\leq c(r)\|A\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}+C(r)\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}.

(6.34)

Finally, applying the lower-order estimate in Lemma
6.1
with

n=1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{X}\mathrm{LO}(u,g)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{r/2}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-r/2})}\leq C(r)\left(\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}\right).

(6.35)

Combining (
6.30
), (
6.32
), (
6.34
), and
(
6.35
), and then repeating the same argument in the lower phase
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-r/2}
,
we get

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{x,z}u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{r/2}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-r/2})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|h\|_{C^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C\|g\|_{C^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+c(r)\|A\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\nabla_{x,z}u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+C(r)\left(\|\nabla_{x,z}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}\right).

(6.36)

It remains to remove the larger
L^{\infty}
-norm on the right-hand side.
Let

\mathcal{A}_{r}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\bigl([r/2,2r]\cup[-2r,-r/2]\bigr).

On
\mathcal{A}_{r}
, the equation is separated from the interface. Applying
the standard interior Lipschitz estimate for divergence-form elliptic
equations with Dini-continuous coefficients separately in

\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[r/2,2r]
and

\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[-2r,-r/2]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{x,z}u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{A}_{r})}\leq C(r)\left(\|\nabla_{x,z}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{3r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-3r})}+\|g\|_{C^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{3r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-3r})}\right).

(6.37)

Since

\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r}=\left(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{r/2}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-r/2}\right)\cup\mathcal{A}_{r},

we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{x,z}u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{2r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-2r})}\leq\|\nabla_{x,z}u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{r/2}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-r/2})}+\|\nabla_{x,z}u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{A}_{r})}.

(6.38)

Substituting (
6.37
) and (
6.38
)
into (
6.36
),
we obtain

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{x,z}u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{r/2}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-r/2})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|h\|_{C^{1,\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C\|g\|_{C^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{3r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-3r})}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+c(r)\|A\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\nabla_{x,z}u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{r/2}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-r/2})}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+C(r)\left(\|\nabla_{x,z}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{3r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-3r})}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{3r}\cup\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{-3r})}\right).

(6.39)

Choose
r\in(0,1)
sufficiently small, depending on

\|A\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, so that

c(r)\|A\|_{\dot{C}^{\log^{\varkappa}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Absorbing the corresponding term into the left-hand side of
(
6.39
), we obtain (
6.26
). The proof is complete.

We conclude the appendix with two elementary singular-integral estimates used in
the proof. The first one is a Hölder estimate for a Calderón–Zygmund type
operator whose kernel depends on the base point.
Let
\mathcal{K}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be such that

\begin{cases}&|\mathcal{K}(x,y)|\leq C|x-y|^{-d},\ \ \ \forall x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},x\neq y,\\
&\int_{|x-y|\geq 10|\alpha|}|\delta_{\alpha}^{x}\mathcal{K}(x,y)||x-y|^{a}dy\lesssim|\alpha|^{a},\ \ \ \forall\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d},a\in(0,1).\end{cases}

(6.40)

For any
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, define the integral operator
\mathsf{T}
as following.

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}h(x,x^{\prime}):=\mathrm{P.V.}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{K}(x,y)h(y,x^{\prime})dy.

(6.41)

We have the following results.

Lemma 6.4

Let
K
satisfy (
6.40
) and

\displaystyle\sup_{0<r_{1}<r_{2}<\infty}\left|\int_{r_{1}<|x-y|<r_{2}}\mathcal{K}(x,y)dy\right|\leq c_{1},

(6.42)

for a constant
c_{1}\geq 0
.
Then for
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
a\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{x,\alpha}\frac{|\mathsf{T}h(x,x)-\mathsf{T}h(x-\alpha,x)|}{|\alpha|^{a}}\lesssim\frac{1}{a(1-a)}\lceil h\rfloor_{a},

(6.43)

where

\lceil h\rfloor_{a}:=\sup_{x,\alpha}\sup_{|z|\leq|\alpha|}\frac{|h(x,x+z)-h(x-\alpha,x+z)|}{|\alpha|^{a}}.

More generally, for
\mathbf{p}=(p_{i})_{i=1}^{d}\in[1,\infty]^{d}
, define the anisotropic Lebesgue space
L^{\mathbf{p}}_{x}=L^{p_{1}}_{x_{1}}\cdots L^{p_{d}}_{x_{d}}
, it holds

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha}\frac{\|\mathsf{T}h(x,x)-\mathsf{T}h(x-\alpha,x)\|_{L^{\mathbf{p}}_{x}}}{|\alpha|^{a}}\lesssim\sup_{\alpha}\sup_{|z|\leq|\alpha|}\frac{\|h(x,x+z)-h(x-\alpha,x+z)\|_{L^{\mathbf{p}}_{x}}}{|\alpha|^{a}}.

(6.44)

Proof.
Let
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and set
\delta=|x_{1}-x_{2}|
. By the cancellation
assumption (
6.42
), we may subtract constants in the principal value
integrals. Hence

\displaystyle|\mathsf{T}h(x_{1},x_{1})-\mathsf{T}h(x_{2},x_{1})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1,2}\left|\int_{|x_{j}-y|\leq 10\delta}\mathcal{K}(x_{j},y)(h(y,x_{1})-h(x_{j},x_{1}))dy\right|+2c_{1}|h(x_{1},x_{1})-h(x_{2},x_{1})|

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\mathbf{1}_{|x_{1}-y|>10\delta}\mathcal{K}(x_{1},y)-\mathbf{1}_{|x_{2}-y|>10\delta}\mathcal{K}(x_{2},y)\right||h(y,x_{1})-h(x_{1},x_{1})|dy

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{|y|\leq 10\delta}\frac{|y|^{a}}{|y|^{d}}dy+\delta^{a}+\int_{|x_{1}-y|>10\delta}|\mathcal{K}(x_{1},y)-\mathcal{K}(x_{2},y)||y-x_{1}|^{a}dy\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.\quad\quad+\int_{9\delta\leq|x_{1}-y|,|x_{2}-y|\leq 10\delta}(|\mathcal{K}(x_{1},y)|+|\mathcal{K}(x_{2},y)|)|y-x_{1}|^{a}dy\right)\lceil h\rfloor_{a}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\delta^{a}\lceil h\rfloor_{a}}{a(1-a)},

which leads to (
6.43
). The estimate (
6.44
) follows similarly by setting
x_{1}=x,x_{2}=x-\alpha
and taking the
L^{\mathbf{p}}
norm in
x
. This completes the proof of the lemma.

The next lemma will be used to pass from estimates with a frozen parameter
to estimates in which the frozen parameter is chosen depending on the
spatial point. In our application the frozen parameter is

\mathbf{b}=\nabla f(x).

Thus, even if a quantity
g(\mathbf{b},\cdot)
is controlled in
L^{2}_{x}

uniformly for each fixed
\mathbf{b}
, we still need a mechanism to control

g(\nabla f(x),x)

as a function of
x
. The following parameter-selection estimate gives
such a mechanism. It only uses the boundedness of the range of

\nabla f
; no regularity of the selecting map
x\mapsto\nabla f(x)
is
needed. The price is Sobolev regularity of
g
in the parameter variable,
above the threshold
d/2
.

Lemma 6.5

Let
k,d\geq 1
, let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
be open, and let

K\Subset U
. Let
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to K
be measurable. Let

l\in\mathbb{N}
satisfy

l>\frac{k}{2}.

Then there exists an open set
U_{0}\Subset U
, with
K\Subset U_{0}
, such
that for every function
g(\beta,x):U\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
with

\nabla_{\beta}^{\alpha}g\in L^{2}_{\beta}(U_{0};L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\qquad|\alpha|\leq l,

one has

\|g(h(\cdot),\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{K,U_{0},l}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq l}\|\nabla_{\beta}^{\alpha}g\|_{L^{2}_{\beta}(U_{0};L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}.

(6.45)

In particular,

\|g(h(\cdot),\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{K,U_{0},l}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq l}\sup_{\beta\in U_{0}}\|\nabla_{\beta}^{\alpha}g(\beta,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

(6.46)

The constant is independent of the measurable selector
h
and of
g
.

Proof.
Choose
U_{0}\Subset U
such that
K\Subset U_{0}
, and choose

\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U_{0})
with
\chi\equiv 1
on a neighborhood of

K
. For a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, apply the Sobolev embedding

H^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
to

\beta\mapsto\chi(\beta)g(\beta,x).

Since
h(x)\in K
and
\chi=1
on
K
, we get

|g(h(x),x)|\leq\|\chi g(\cdot,x)\|_{L^{\infty}_{\beta}}\lesssim\|\chi g(\cdot,x)\|_{H^{l}_{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{k})}.

By Leibniz’ rule and the fact that
\chi
is fixed,

\|\chi g(\cdot,x)\|_{H^{l}_{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{k})}\lesssim_{K,U_{0},l}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq l}\|\nabla_{\beta}^{\alpha}g(\cdot,x)\|_{L^{2}_{\beta}(U_{0})}.

Therefore

|g(h(x),x)|\lesssim_{K,U_{0},l}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq l}\|\nabla_{\beta}^{\alpha}g(\cdot,x)\|_{L^{2}_{\beta}(U_{0})}.

Squaring, integrating in
x
, and using that the number of multiindices

|\alpha|\leq l
is finite, we obtain

\displaystyle\|g(h(\cdot),\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{K,U_{0},l}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq l}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\nabla_{\beta}^{\alpha}g(\cdot,x)\|_{L^{2}_{\beta}(U_{0})}^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim_{K,U_{0},l}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq l}\|\nabla_{\beta}^{\alpha}g\|_{L^{2}_{\beta}(U_{0};L^{2}_{x})}^{2}.

Taking square roots gives (
6.45
). Since
U_{0}
has
finite measure,

\|\nabla_{\beta}^{\alpha}g\|_{L^{2}_{\beta}(U_{0};L^{2}_{x})}\leq|U_{0}|^{1/2}\sup_{\beta\in U_{0}}\|\nabla_{\beta}^{\alpha}g(\beta,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{x}},

which gives (
6.46
). The proof is complete.
